Available now for only $199." Available now for only $299." 


Elegantly engineered, the S600 and $800 printers represent a major leap in ink jet technology. Showcases for innovation, these 
printers offer an unrivaled combination of quality, speed and cost-saving performance. The breathtaking quality is due to Canon's advanced 
MicroFine Droplet Technology" an innovation that produces ink droplets just 5 picoliters in size and places them with ultimate precision — 
without sacrificing speed. The result is brilliant 2400 x 1200 color dpi prints that will last up to 25 years without fading* And since Canon's 
PhotoRecord™ software is included in the box, printing photos has never been easier: Besides the quality and speed 
superiority, it's Canon's exclusive money-saving individual ink tank system that really sets these printers apart from 
the pack. With separate ink tanks, you no longer have to replace the entire cartridge when a single color, like blue, 
runs out. You simply replace the blue. It’s technology that makes sense. For more details about Canon digital 
imaging solutions, visit us at www.usa.canon.com/consumer or call /800-OK-CANON. Canon KNOW HOW”

fast-forward skewering of Silicon Alley, Wall Street, and early 21st-century cybercapitalism. 
Over at YIL.com, we're serializing the entire novel, releasing a new chapter each week. And 
we know what you're thinking. So what? This is the Web. Do something new, something 
exciting, something different, something interesting, something visionary. Push some 
boundaries, for Pete's sake. Okay, you want boundaries? You want pushing? Exit Strategy 
is the world's first open-source novel. That means you, the reader, are invited to help Artificial 
create itin an ongoing, dynamic process that could very well redefine the concept of book 
as we know it (and if not, we'll at least have a lot of fun trying). Get the whole scoop over cua 
at yil.com/exitstrategy; then give us the lowdown on how the future really happened. Ieee MAES

EDITOR’S NOTE 


An Amazing, Hair-Raising Ride... 


A MAGAZINE'S COVER LINES ARE MEANT TO BE PRO- 
vocative and maybe, on occasion, even a wee bit hyper- 
bolic. So, this month's “Five Years That Changed the 
World.” Truth? Or more hype? 

I's a reasonable question, especially in light of the most- 
definitely-true pop of the hot-air spheroid known as the 


Internet bubble and its multi- 


billion-dollar paper fortunes. 
A case can be made that this 
fine little how-de-do was due 
in large part to the influence 
of one thing; hype. 

But as the chroniclers of 
the Net’s people, rather than 
its entrepreneurs and profi- 
teers, we at Yahoo! Internet Life 
have a fair record for not over- 
stating the case, So, come again: 
changed the world? 

Yes, I believe so. In the years 
since our launch, at the dawn 
of the Web, lives have been 
changed in almost every re- 
spect. This anniversary issue 


is a kind of yearbook chroni 
cling the effect of those times. 

Yet it comes at a moment of 

immense confusion about the Net. After all the inflated claims 
and deflated portfolios, there’s a sense of disenchantment in 
the mainstream press, as if to suggest it was all an illusion. 

It was, of course, nothing of the sort. The changes— 
Web sites and search engines, e-mail, MP3s and Napster-like 
file-sharing, and on and on—were profound and will last, 
woven into our everyday lives. So enjoy this issue for what it 
is: a celebration of the amazing, often hair-raising years we 
passed through together. 

At Oscar time we put up with movie stars who thank their 
mothers, their agents, and their spouses (if they can remem- 
ber their names). Now | ask you to put up with me while I 
gratefully attach a few names to this issue's main themes. 


Yahoo! Internet Life > September 2001 


We begin with an overview of what our Net ethics colum- 
nist thinks we've learned these five years; that would be 
ANGELA GUNN, one of the original Y-Lifers. After that, you'll 
find a special State of the Net 2001 section, put together 
by senior editor CREE McCREE, in lieu of our Web Guide 
this month. Our fashion spread—with an e-mail twist—was 
photographed by MOSHE 
BRAKHA, and produced and 
written by the style team of 
WARREN CHRISTOPHER, GAIL 
HENRY, and MEAGHAN O'NEILL. 
Another Y-Life pioneer, RON 
BEL BRUNO, put together the 
self-retrospective we call “This 
Was Our Y-Life.” Our list of the 
Net's greatest hits and loudest 
flops was compiled by tech- 
nology editor DON WILLMOTT 
and senior editor BILGE EBIRI. 

The issue's party favor— 

“The 17 People (and One Sock 
Puppet) Who Shaped the 
Net”—was helmed editorially 
by ROB BERNSTEIN, and finessed 
into being by photo artist 
SANJAY KOTHARI, with assis- 
tance from associate photo 
editor MIKE MANDEL. The next section, in which noted au- 
thors reflect on their defining “Net Moments,” was spear- 
headed by executive editor LARRY SMITH, with assists from 
senior associate editor JEREMY CAPLAN. We end our inqui- 
sition into past and present with Modern Humorist's wry 
look forward: a preview of our 10th-anniversary issue. 


So thanks for the memories so far, and I hope you'll join 
us for the ride to the next five-year mark. I promise (no hype!) 
that it’s going to be a really, really interesting time. 


BARRY GOLS wn Cots EDITOR IN CHIEF

“The People’s 
Net,” by Dou- 
glas Rushkoff 


78], was simply 
the most dead- 
on, astute article 
I've read about 
the post—dot- 
com gold-rush era. I’ve always 
- felt media coverage about the 
“tise and fall” of the Internet was 
off target. Perhaps now we can 
rediscover what originally ap- 
pealed to us about the Net 
namely, that it is a powerful 


means to communicate and 


create a global community. 
After reading the article, I re- 
flected on the success of my per- 
sonal Web site 
not on revenue or a juggernaut 
IPO, but on connecting with 
people. Rushkoff is right: The

uccess based 


Internet is back. However, for 
many of us, it never went away.

BUSINESS CLASS 
‘Tivo cheers for Mr. Rushkoff’s 
“The People’s Net,” an excel- 
lent cover story that provided 
a veteran's insight and keen un- 
derstanding. Maybe that’s why 
I was so disappointed with the 
author’s utopian conclusion. 
Perhaps an attempt to neatly 
wrap up the story, it came off 
as too convenient. Furthermore, 
I disagree with Rushkoff's at- 
tempt to paint e-commerce as 
“an investment charade.” Please. 
True, big bad business 
changed an unspoiled and close- 
knit community into a different 


kind of cyberculture. Moreover, 


I agree that e-commerce had a 
negative influence on aspects of 


“It can download entire novels, surf the Net, do e-mail, get real- 
time stock quotes, shop for groceries, buy me a new car, and cook 
my dinner. But mostly | just use it to see what time it is.”

the Net, but one cannot dispute 


its defining role in spreading the 
Internet worldwide. While it 


originally grew out of a shared 
vision and collaborative imagi- 
nation, the Internet matured and 
gained mass acceptance and cred- 
ibility through e-commerce.

FOR THE RECORD 

Joanna Chen’s overall informa- 
tive and interesting article mis- 
led readers ona couple of points. 
First, the reporter repeats with- 
out challenge the Palestinian 
claim that Isam Judeh was 
tortured to death by Israelis, 


“including some particularly | 


brutal treatment with a red-hot 
iron.” Yet the facts are emphat- 
ically otherwise. Physicians for 
Human Rights conducted an 


exhaustive investigation into the 


circumstances of Judeh’s death 
and concluded that he died in a 
car accident. Chen should have 
made this point clear. 

Also, Chen's identification 
of Palestinian violence as “vi- 
olent demonstration against 
_ Israeli occupation” miscasts 
_ the nature of the recent crisis. 
Obscured in such language, for 
one, is the fact that nearly 100 
percent of Palestinians in the 
West Bank and Gaza live un- 
der the Palestinian Authority, 
not under Israeli rule. 

TAMAR STERNTHAL 
Senior Research Analyst 
CAMERA: The Committee for 
Accuracy in Middle East 
Reporting in America 
@camera.org

Of course you've never heard of Nicola Iacocca or Pasquale Leno. But before their descendants be- 
came titans of the auto industry and late-night television, these men arrived as immigrants at New 


York's Ellis Island. Now you can unearth the details of others’ transatlantic passage at the American 
ISLAN D Family Immigration History Center [ellisislandrecords.orgl, thanks to the Herculean development 
efforts of the Church of Latter-day Saints, which through this project continues its commitment to 


genealogical research. As you hunt for Great-Grandpa in a database of more than 22 million arrivals 
between 1892 and 1924, you'll be among the n ; the site greeted 1.9 million visitors in its first full

GOVERNOR ‘O° > 


A new study from the Markle Founda- 


tion reveals that Americz 


s want tighter 
Net regulation. The report's respondents 
expressed an almost equal preference for 


private and government regulation to 
curb pornography and violence online. 
When asked who should serve on a hy- 
pothetical Internet Board of Governors, 
however, the group was more surefooted, 
casting a strong preference for Pope John 
Paul I1, Oprah Winfrey, and Bill Gates. 
So? Enforcing Net regulations is 
herently problematic. And not even 


Oprah could or, we suspect, would 
trump the First Amendment. 


HERE'S LOOKING AT YOU 
Think twice before placing an online 
personal ad from your office PC. A re- 
cent Privacy Foundation study reveals 
that 35 percent of the nation's 40 
million at-work surfers are being mon- 
itored by their companies for inap- 
propriate Net use. Why? Tracking is 
easy, cheap, and can detect the swap- 
ping of dirty jokes, salacious pictures, 
and other lawsuit-inducing items. 

So? Get a copy of your company’s Net, 
policy—if it has one—and surf only 
those sites you wouldn't mind show- 
ing anyone else af work.

NAPSTER’S DARK DAYS 
Two weeks after Napster went offline in 
early July, reports began to surface that 
the file-sharing network planned to 
abandon MP3 in favor of a secure pro- 
prietary format as it retooled to accom- 
modate a new fee-based service. By the 
time Napster went dark, the average 
number of songs being shared by each 
user had plunged from 220 to 1.5. One 
bright spot: Cable-TV programmer Starz 
Encore is considering a dramatization 
of Napster's rise and fall. 

So? As Napster refugees flock to such 


record. Joining such recent inductees 


as dotcom and webcam are geek mainstays 


cybersex and spam, as well as the au courant 
MP3. For now, however, you'll have to 
settle for viewing these new entries in 
the dictionary’s subscription-based Web 


version: The publisher won't be releas 
ing a printed update of the 1928 edi- 
tion for about 10 years. 

So? Our beloved gibberish is now 
simply English.

NEXT: BAR-CODE TATTOOS? 

The European Union may begin asking 
its [SPs to keep records of customers’ Net 
doings (from surfing to e-mail) for as long 
as seven years. Condemned by ICANN 
chairman and Net elder statesman Vint 
Cerf as impractical, this move is also at 


odds with established privacy laws.

ARE YOUR FRIENDS TRYING TO REACH 
YOU? PUT CALL-WAITING ON YOUR PC 


Ifyou have just one phone line, you know 
the problem: You can’t receive calls while 
you're on the Web. Sure, you can use an 
answering machine or service, but you won't 
get your messages until you're offline. 
That's where Net-based call-waiting 
comes in. It lets you know via an on-screen 
alert when you're receiving a phone call: 


Your local phone company redirects in- 


SERVICE 


PLATFORMS 
Windows 95, 98, NT 4.0, | 


Click! > home&S&office 


)LD THE PHONE 


coming calls to the service's computer, and 
it immediately sends notification to your 
screen. With the click ofa mouse, you can 
ask the caller to try back later or leave a 
voice message. All the services have Caller 
1D, but only two letyou pick up the phone 
and speak to callers immediately, and 
only BuzMe includes that feature in its fee. 
Costs are reasonable, but remember that 
you'll also have to pay your phone com- 


Dall Forwarding, which typ- 


pany for Busy 


ically costs about $2 per month. TROY DREIER

Show You Care 


Generations of moms have 
Naam filled shoe boxes with cook- 
ies, Bazooka bubble gum, 
socks, and other necessi- 
ties and mailed them off to their kids 
at camp or in college. The tradition 
lives on at CarePackages.com, where 
you can save time by ordering up a 
quirky prefab care package in one of 
20 categories. It will typically feature 
something plush, something funny, 
something useful, and something 
edible. The $35 Moving Relief Kit, for 
example, has a flashlight, duct tape, 
soap, chocolate, and a Blockbuster gift 
certificate, among other items, while 
the $28 Freshman package includes 
12 gifts, such as stamps, a prepaid 
phone card, 150 paper clips, and some 
Oreos. DON WILLMOTT

TUNE IN TO NET RADIO 

Thousands of radio stations around 
the world are streaming their signals 
onto the Net, and there are thou- 
sands of ways to find and listen to

an Internet radio player that lets you 
locate and flip between stations by 
just hitting Play. Its directory features 
stations from more than 100 coun- 
tries, and all musical genres are rep- 
resented. You can create your own 
playlist of favorite stations, too. Free 
to try; $20 to register and $10 to add 
a backup disk.

ENGINEERED TO BE 
ALNIOST PSYCHIC. 


Introducing a car so technologically advanced, it 
can sense trouble and begin to adjust for it before 
the driver even notices there’s a problem. It’s the 
6-cylinder 212-horsepower Subaru Outback VDC. 
The VDC stands for Vehicle Dynamics Control, 
a highly intelligent stability system that’s designed 
to help prevent loss of control due to conditions like 
oversteer, understeer, wheel spin or vehicle drift. 
Ingeniously coupled with full-time Subaru All-Wheel 
Drive, it rivals systems found in vehicles costing 


thousands more. 


Steering angle sensor 


Lateral g-sensor 


Yaw rate sensor 


-aad 


Wheel speed sensor 


The heart of VDC is a sophisticated series 
of sensors that continually monitor steering angle, 
wheel speed, brake pressure, yaw rate and lateral 
g-forces. The instant a difference is detected between 
the driver's intended direction of travel and the path 


the car is actually taking, VDC takes corrective action. 


‘The ABC's of Safety: Air bags. Buckle up. Children in back. 
“At maximum output 


Momentary brake pressure may be applied to 
individual wheels. The All-Wheel Drive system may 
redistribute the amount of power between the wheels. 
Even the engine’s output may be momentarily 
reduced. Before the driver even realizes that loss of 
control is impending, any or all of these measures may 
be automatically applied to help restore directional 
stability. It's almost as if the car has a sixth sense. 

In fact, in every sense the Outback VDC is a 
remarkable vehicle. With tactile luxuries like a leather- 
trimmed, 8-way power driver's seat. A mahogany and 
leather steering wheel by Momo? And a state-of-the-art 
200-watt’ sound system built exclusively for Subaru by 
Mclntosh®. Working together, McIntosh and Subaru 
engineers placed 11 speakers in 7 strategic locations 
so the audio quality would be specifically tuned to 
the car’s unique acoustics. 

The Outback VDC from Subaru. It is truly a 
phenomenon in the world of automotive engineering. 
And we have a feeling you're going to love it. To find 


out more, come visit us at www.subaru.com.

BUYING SCHOOL SUPPLIES 


OLD 


Back to school means new notebooks, binders, pencils, erasers, 


WAY 


and, this year, a protractor! (A letter from the school included 
a shopping list.) At a local drugstore, we loaded our basket. Total: 
$31.65. But no protractors. The grocery next door had one—for $2. 


TIME ELAPSED 30 minutes 


COMMENTS Elementary school is a breeze compared 


with what's to come, as I know from my college-age offspring. 


NET WAY 


Hl Are there school supplies online? Are they any cheaper? I 
checked. 4SchoolSupplies.com has a great feature: If your 
child’s teacher puts a shopping list online, the site will fill it for 
you. However, my total order would have been $8 higher, plus 
asmall shipping fee. 


) ) Another site, SchoolKidz.com, has a wonderful idea: “grade 
ready” boxes. If the selections match your kids’ teachers’ 


lists, it’s a time- and money-saver. 


TIME ELAPSED 20 minutes 


COMMENTS If shopping at the local store weren't so sim- 


ple, the Net might be a preferable alternative. 


OLD WAY 


Needing a visa quickly for a trip to China, I called the Chi- 
nese consulate in San Francisco. When I finally got through, 
arecording explained the procedure for getting a visa by mail. 


Headed to the local office. Passport, photos, and cash in 


hand, I filled out the required form and waited in line. 


x After dropping off the material, | returned to pick up the 
visa three days later. (The rush cost an extra $20.) 


TIME ELAPSED Three hours of busy signals, 30 


minutes in the office, and then three days of waiting 


COMMENTS Once I got to the office, it went surpris- 


ingly smoothly. No complaints about that. 


Yahoo! Internet Life > September 2001 


GETTING A TRAVEL VISA IN A HURRY | 


NET WAY 


I needed another visa. Consulate General of the People's Repub- 
lic of China in San Francisco [www.chinaconsulatesf.orgl greeted me 
with the Chinese national anthem and offered downloadable forms. 


If I'd had more than 30 days before my trip, I could have 

done the whole thing by mail, using the forms | downloaded. 
But I was again in a hurry. Went to the passport office in San 
Francisco with the forms and other required material. The pro- 
cedure was the same as in the Old Way. 


TIME ELAPSED Three minutes online, 30 minutes at 


the visa office, and the same three days for processing. 


COMMENTS There were no busy signals, and the infor- 


mation was easier to navigate online.

BIG BUNNY A giant, man-eating rabbit 
lurks in the woods, luring kind, clueless 
little kids with stories about zombie 
birds and evil turnips. Presumably, he's 
hoping to eat the kids himself, since 
he offers them seats inside a giant hot- 
dog bun and exhorts them at the end 
of each episode to eat lots of fatty food. 
Both cute and caustic, this animated se- 
ties out of UCLA has a South Park-ian 
dementia that will please even the 
most jaded of viewers. And is that a 
hint of Henry Kissinger we hear in Big 
Bunny's voice? BE. Inibblebox. 
com/public/index2.htmll 


WE ARE ROBOTS Positing robots as 
contemporary Everymen (on a fraction 
of the budget of Steven Spielberg's 
A.l,), each episode in this slight 
comedy series profiles the travails of a 
different character, usually defined by a 
single characteristic. Angrybot rages 
against the world and flips us off, while 
the premiere show's Sad Robot mopes 
about nihilistically. Modestly conceived 
and fitfully amusing, the confessional- 
style episodes have agreeably simple 
animation, but the one-note humor can 
sometimes wear out its welcome. AF

THE ANGELS OF RUSSIA 

By Patricia le Roy 

Say hello to the thinking person's spy 
thriller. Against the backdrop of 
glasnost and Mikhail Gorbachev's 
splintering Soviet Union, a fledgling 
romance between a Pushkin-loving 
Parisian and a Russian dissident 
morphs into a domino line of 
betrayals, forcing inhabitants of both 
East and West to adjust their illusions 
to accommodate the vagaries of the 
teal world, Le Roy could use an editor 
to tame her flowery language, but she 
certainly knows how to craft a gripping 
plot. In 1998, this was the first online 
work to be submitted for nomination 
for Britain's prestigious Booker Prize. 
And it isn't just good for an e-book.

KEEP YOUR CHILDREN OUT OF 
HARM'S WAY ONLINE 


It’s inevitable: If children have free rein on the home com- 


puter, they will stumble across online pornography (acci- 


dentally, of course), With the Web's seediest s 


es only a 
click away via popular search engines and directories, proac- 
tive parents need tools to steer kids clear of online porn. 
Searchopolis [searchopolis.com] uses a blacklist of sites 
to filter queries and offers a directory of handpicked 
links in such subjects as math, social studies, and language 
arts. Youngsters can also hit Super Snooper [supersnooper. 
coml, another tool that returns kid-friendly links. 
Many portals offer filtering options, too. AltaVista Fam- 
ily Filter [altavista.com/sites/search/ffset] blocks search 


results related to drugs, gambling, hate speech, sexually 


explicit mat 


the top of the site’s home page. The more advanced Lycos 
SearchGuard [searchguard.lycos.coml lets you control the 


medium that gets blocked (for example, Lycos chat), 


SearchGuard and Family Filter even allow parents to 


set up a password for enabling or disabling the filters. 


Keep in mind that kid-friendly filters and directories 


These tools are just a first line of defense, 


E LEAST TIME 


EDITED BY ROB BERNSTEIN 


are far from perfec 


and they may allow some objectionable content to seep through. 


Filters may inadvertently block useful sites, too—such as pages on 


designed especially for kids. Its Internet Explorer—style interface 


will get children used to surfing while restricting them to a data- 


base of approximately 320 useful sites (parents have the option 


breast cancer awareness. For these reasons, you may want to con 


sider installing ChiBrow [childrenbrowser.coml, a free Web browser 


of adding more). ChiBrow also offers access to monitored chat- 


rooms and lets kids send e-cards to friends.

SEARCH ASSISTANT

This useful Internet Explorer add-on 
opens a frame on the left-hand side 


of your browser to display thumbnail 


images of search results, allowing you_ 


to see whether a link is relevant be- 
fore clicking on it. Girafa also offers 
quick access to 20 different search en- 
gines and can eliminate redundant 
results. The utility is free and works 
on systems running Windows 95, 98, 
NT, ME, or 2000.

FUN WITH FILES: THE INS AND OUTS OF OPENING, 
COMPRESSING, AND SENDING TROUBLESOME ATTACHMENTS 


(enconaaere A 00 (7656 K8 /396.03KB [19062001 /12365¢ 


OD Jove obstinate files | 


When you double-click a file, your computer will almost al- 
ways launch it ina specific program. For instance, on most PCs, 
files that end in the three-letter extension .doc open in Microsoft 
Word. And files that end in .mp3 probably open in a digital audio 
program, such as Real Jukebox or Windows Media Player. 

However, you may occasionally download a file from the Net 
or receive a file via e-mail that utterly confounds your computer 
and will not open at all. What's the problem? In all probability, 
your system does not have a registered program that can read or 
open the file. When this happens, turn to Irfanview 32 [stud4.tuwien.ac. 
at/~e9227474/english.html, a free, all-in-one viewer that can open 
more than 50 different kinds of graphics, audio, and video files— 
even those with obscure three-letter extensions such as .pex or .iff- 

Another problem you may encounter when opening a file is 
its insistence on launching in a specific program. For example, 
when you open an HTML document, your PC may choose to launch 
Internet Explorer instead of Netscape Navigator, or vice versa. There 
are several ways to work around this problem, but perhaps the 
simplest fix is to install PC Magazine's Freedom of Association 
[zdnet.com/downloads/stories/info/0,,77392,.htmll. This free PC- 
only program lets you change file associations at will and take 
control over domineering computer software. 


Fle Diectowtocent Fie. _| 


[e:\..\program\aim\sounds 
T Show full pathname of file to buddy 
Entet a descriation of the file so your buddy can decide 
whether to accept it 


[Here's a file ofcoel sounds for your 


computer. Enjoy @ @ @ 


} OPENING THE 
| X-FILES. 
Esa Launch tricky or 
‘When you send a file your buddy may be able mysterious files 
demi yous adktess, which is a potential security risk with Irfanview 
Make sure you know and trust your buddy. (far left); send 
q : giant files using 
>) _ sed | Eee AOL Instant 
Sees §Messenger. 


0? squeeze attachments 


Other kinds of files that can bring your computing to a screech- 
ing halt are archived or compressed files. They usually end in 
.sip but may also end with .tar, .gz, .tgs, .uue, .xxe, .hgx (BinHex), 
amme, mim, .cab, or jar. To open an archived file, you'll require a 
program capable of decompressing it, such as Aladdin Systems’ 
free Stufflt Expander [aladdinsys.com] for the PC or Mac. And if 
you need a compression tool for, say, shrinking and e-mailing a 
large movie file to a friend, try using FreeZip [members.ozemail. 
com.au/-nulifetv/freezip]. The program requires Windows 95, 98, 
2000, NT, or ME. Other popular archive utilities include WinZip 
[winzip.coml and PKZip [pkware.coml, which are free to try and 
cost $29 and $34, respectively, to buy. 


OD 3 slice and splice 


Not everyone has a rewriteable CD drive, so what to do if you still 
use 1.44MB floppies? Download a dedicated program that will al- 
low you to split a file into several pieces, One of the best free “file 
splitters” is Kazushi Oenoki’s File Shredder [kazusoft.pair.com/software/ 
shredder]; it works with systems running Windows 95, 98, or NT. 
Another great way to transfer large files is via AOL Instant 
Messenger. Yes, AIM lets you exchange a lot more than idle chat. 
Simply open an IM window and click File/Send File. LOU DOLINAR

MASK YOUR TRUE NAME IN E-MAIL; 


SAVE QUICKTIME MOVIES TO YOUR HARD DRIVE; KEEP TRACK OF MP3s 


Q)? | don’t want my real name to appear in the “From” field in my 
outgoing e-mail messages. Is there a way to change it? 

A)? Have something to hide? It’s pretty simple to change the 
name recipients see when they open your messages. In Microsoft 
Outlook Express, click the Tools menu and choose Accounts. This 
will launch the Internet Accounts window. Click the Mail tab, and 
then double-click the account youd like to modify. In the User In- 
formation area, change your name. In Netscape Mail, click the Edit 
menu and choose Preferences. This will launch the Preferences 
window. In the Category frame, click Mail and Newsgroups and 
then Identity. In the “Your name” field, modify your information. 


Q)) QuickTime will not let me save movies to my hard disk. What gives? 
A») Calm down—there’s nothing wrong with your software. 
Apple Computer built this feature only into QuickTime Pro, the 
pay version of its popular software; it costs $30 for Windows and 


Mac users. That said, I do know of a sneaky workaround that 


will allow you to save movies to your hard drive at no cost. Open 
your free version of QuickTime Player and go to Edit/Prefer- 


ences/QuickTime Preferences. This will launch a QuickTime 


Settings window. Check the box that reads “Save movies in disk 
cache.” This will instruct QuickTime to save a version of every 
movie file you play in your disk cache, where you can fish it out. 
If you use Internet Explorer, cached files are usually saved in 
C:/Windows Temporary Internet Files. Netscape Navigator users will 
have to poke around in C:/Program Files/Netscape/ Users. 


Q)>? I have trouble keeping track of the hundreds of tunes I've loaded 
onto my computer. What's the best way to organize it all? 

A)? I've instructed Napster and my sundry MP3-related soft- 
ware to place all music files downloaded from the Net or ripped 
from a CD into a single folder on my desktop. This way I have 
one-click access to tunes on my hard drive. If that sounds like too 
much work, then you might consider downloading MusicMatch 
Jukebox 6.1 [musicmatch.coml. Unlike most digital audio players, 
MusicMatch can scour your hard drive for MP3 files, whether 
they're sitting on your desktop, buried somewhere on your C: drive, 
or both. It can also sort these files by track name, album name, 
and more. Click to Options/Music Library/Search and Add Track(s) 


from All Drives to access this feature in MusicMatch 6. 


You don't have to log in or connect to 
the Net to access some of AOL's most 


important features. Right-click on the access th 


on inyo stem tray to launch 


a composition window, address book, 
filing cabinet, or calendar. You can also 


idea to password-protect 
sensitive e-mail messages 
stored in your filing cabinet.

course, anyone using your. 
computer can also access any 
of these features without 
knowing your screen name 
or password, so it's a good

"WEB DAYS' WILL HAVE 
A HAPPY ENDING 


Should this gala anniv te of Yahoo! Internet Life be 
the occasion for a Net obituary or a celebration? The Inter- 


ry i 


net is in economic crisis, but its brilliance and audacity con- 
tinuéjto amaze me. The stories of its decline are stories about 
money, nGttechnology or communication, If millions of in- 
vestors felt compelled to throw their cash insanely at Inter- 
net investments that made zero economic sense, is that an 
indictment of the Internet, or a demonstration that they were 
greedy herd animals? 

I think it’s time to stop reading about the Internet on the 
financial pages. If your interest is in using the Internet, not 
getting rich from it, the stock price of Cis 


0 is insignificant. 
n my life, the Web and e-mail play crucial roles every single 
day, If I had to replace e-mail with the telephone and the fax 
machine, I would go mad. If I didn’t 


rave the research resources of the Web 


available to me, I would accomplish 
half 
Saigon Si 


much work. If I couldn’t order 


zzle Sauce at amoment’s no 


tice, | might have to move to Saigon. 

And without the Internet Movie 
Database, | would have to return to 
the Dark Ages of movie press kits and fe) 


publicity handouts as a source of in- 
formation. | actually wonder if I could 
find the energy, the patience, or the 
will to do my job without the Web; I 
did it once, but how? 

A lot of us were using e-mail, FTP 
sites, CompuServe, AOL, or whatever a long time before the 
Web came along. When we all crowded aboard the first 


browsers, there was an electrifying moment when we real- 


ized that this, at last, was the interface the Internet had been 
yearning for, It was way cool. I bookmarked New Site of the 


Day and actually visited it. 


Some investors allowed their personal joy in the medium 


inancial judgment. “If this is such a gas,” they 


to cloud their 
thought, “it’s sure to make me a lot of money.” But there 
was no sound economic model under that thinking. The 
Internet and all its suppliers and content providers existed 


Yahoo! Internet Life > September 2001 


Now that no 
one wants to throw 
money at the 
Net anymore, 
the owner-operators 
of Web sites 
will have to earn 
or beg for money like 
anyone else 


in a warp in economic space. Nobody owned it, nobody paid 
for it, nobody ran it, everybody visited it. 
There is a new documentary titled Startup.com about the 


rise and fall of a Web company. I can think of another re- 
cent documentary that more accurately mirrors the first five 
years of the Web, That would be Dark Days, by Marc Singer, 
about the people who live in the railroad tunnels under Man 

s from 


hattan. Down there beneath the city, they build hous 
plywood and cardboard, and supply them with running wa- 


ter and electricity tapped (that is, stolen) from the city’s 
pipes and wires. Some of the houses surprise us; they have 


Ils. 
The Web essentially spent its first years living in tunnels 


second stor shower s 


kitchens, TVs, household pets 


under the economy. Web sites lived off the free transmission 
of their images. The pipes and wires 
they tapped were paid for by the gov- 
ernment, people thought, when they 
thought about it at all. 

The difference between Dark Days 
and “Web Days’ 
Singer’s documentary go up top to 
panhandle and search through Dump: 


is that the heroes in 


sters for food, while the heroes of the 
Web found that people were shovel- 
ing truckloads of money down through 
the manholes. Talented workers even 
gave up jobs on the surface to work in 
the tunnels under the economy. 
Dark Days has a happy ending. Most 


of the people in the tunnels are relocated into subsidized hous- 


ing aboveground. “Web Days” will have one, too. Now that no 
one wants to throw money at the Internet anymore, the owner- 


operators of Web sites will have to come up top and earn or beg 


for money like anyone No more overnight millionaires. 
The Internet Bubble has been compared to the ‘Tulip Craze, 
when 17th-century investors bid the price of Dutch bulbs 
to insane heights. Both bubbles burst. The collapse of the In- 
ternet economy was inevitable, and clears the way for sane 
and reasonable rebuilding. Good news; There are more tulips 


in the world than ever before. =

Remember the last tie some syrupy canned voice asked 
you for the last four digits of your Social Security number? 
Or the last time you entered the entire sequence into an 
online order form? 

Proyiding,your Social Security number to a business or a 
government agency is supposed to protect both parties by 
helping them to ascertain that it’s really you initiating a trans- 
action. But foras little as 25 bucks, anyone can buy your num- 
ber on the Internet and pretend to be you. 

Who would have thought that the lowly Social Security 
number, whose power lay largely dormant for more than half 
a centuty, could become suchia scourge in the Internet Age? 


Originally, a number was issued to each individual as a way 
for the government to track what it owed retirees. Nowadays, 
at least online, your Social Security 
number is often the key to who you 
are, Thus, it has become both a vital 
tool for servicing your most discreet 


consumer needs—and a weapon that 


others can easily turn against you. 

The issue may not seem important 
to those who haven't experienced the 
nig] itmare of seeing someone run up 
enormous bills in their name, and of 
watching their credit record become 
sullied for years, But identity theft 


is the leading growth crime in the 
U.S., upsetting the lives of a reported 
700,000 people last year. And the 
successful theft of a person’s identity almost always starts with 
some crook’s getting hold of a Social Security number. That's 
not so difficult to do, given that your number is revealed on 
myriad government documents and on credit applications, 
and that the information is legally available online under the 
terms of the 


Sredit and Reporting Act. 

This needs to change. And it will change if Rep. Clay Shaw 
(R-Fla.) and Senators Dianne Feinstein (D- Calif.) and Judd 
Gregg (R-N.H.) get their w 


. The three legislators want to 


make sure the Social Security number is used only for its orig- 


inal purpose, and to ban its use as a de facto national ID card.

almost always. 
starts with a Social

SPRIWACY WATCH 


If their legislation is enacted, your Social Security number 
wouldn’t appear on your driver's license, or in the informa- 
tion that identifies your bank account. And the guy in the video 
store wouldn't be able to demand that you turn over the 
number as a general identifier, although the IRS would. 
Despite its bipartisan sponsorship, however, the law will 
ise the mar- 


have a hell of a time getting passed. That's bec: 


keting community—which includes the nation’s most power- 


ful financial interests—finds it just too inconvenient to keep 
tabs on you any other way. 
I say, “Tough.” It’s time to set limits if we want to keep our 


the 


identities secure. The law must be cl 


you entered into 
a deal with the government to use the Social Security number 


for a specific purpose, and that everyone else who wants to get 


theft 


in on the act must back off or face stiff 
criminal penalties. 

What greater warning do we need 
than the words of the one guy who un- 
derstands most thoroughly the menace 
of identity theft? Arguing in support of 
tough new legislation, John G. Huse Jr., 
the Social Security Administration's in- 
spector general, has said that misuse 
of Social Security numbers has become 


a“national crisis.” He has also noted that 


possession of a Social Security num- 
ber confers the “power to engage in fi- 
nancial transactions, power to obtain 


personal information, power to create 


or commandeer identities,” all of which “makes it a valuable 


sset and one that is subject to limitless abuse.” 


Online or off, | urge you never to turn over your Social Se- 
curity number unless the transaction is connected with the num- 
ber’s original purpose. Anyone demanding that you cough it up 
as a condition of doing business ought to be prevented from do- 
ing business at all. Soon enough, merchants and agencies will be 


asking for our genetic profiles. For now, let’s reclaim our own 


social security by keeping our numbers to ourselve

Still Bianca After 
All These Years 


The recently announced hiatus of the 
early indie Web sites Suck and Feed.com 
got me thinking about Bianca [bianca. 
coml, arguably the first Web-based com- 
munity. Born Bianca's Smut Shack 


in 1994, it was started by a couple 
of friends who wanted to re-create 
the atmosphere of their Chicago 
home, a funky flophouse full of 
friends who buzzed in and out, do: 

ing all the things people do in a vi- 
brant shared space. The site’s motto? 
“Bianca loves you!” —whoever she 
is, whoever you are, and regardless 
of whether you've come to chat 
about tattoos or technology, med- 


icine or politics or even 


The downturn in online ad- 
vertising has been a crusher for the 
site, which actually announced it 
would close before finding a money- 
saving tech tweak that kept it afloat. 
But Bianca has been a survivor 
through every step of the Net’s evo- 
lution: the early obsession for ob- 
session's sake, the dreams of a big 
payday, the brush with death, the 
buyout, the bailout. Italso survived 


a lawsuit by RadioShack, which, 


uh, claimed to own the word shack. 
Atsome point, founders Dave Thau 

and Chris Miller realized what now 

seems so obvious: Online and offline 


go hand in hand. So Bianca harvested 


the cozy vibe of its virtual world and 
manifested it in the physical one 
Through parties across the country— 
full of grilled cheeses, big cushions, the

that seats 746. “I work for God, but I 
also have to work for myself,” Ton- 


wuru says, glancing around at the in- 


ternational crowd assembled in the 


as 
18,300-square-foot café—the largest 
Web-ready hangout in the world. 
No one seems to notice Tonwuru, 
despite his breaking the unspoken code 
of librarylike hush, In just one of the 
café's 61 aisles, surfers invoke French, 
Russian, Spanish, and German as they 
dig into their keyboards, staring straight 
ahead into whatever it is they're look- 
ing for. “I’m usually here until 2 or 3 
A.M. every night,” he says, “but some 
of these people never seem to leave.”

AMERI 


All Squid, 
No Ink 


When videographer Scott Beale fi- 


nally made the pilgrimage to the Burn- 
ing Man [burningman.com] festival in 
1995, it blew his mind. Drifting among 
the campsites, machine wars, theme 
parties, and environmental art in- 
stallations, he felt he'd found the 


home-away-from-home he had 


dreamed of ever since leaving Day- 
ton for San Francisco. 

“L thought, What made this hap- 
pen?” says Beale, 33. “There’s so 
much cool stuff there, it can’t be 
only happening at Burning Man. 
It must going on all the time and 
people just don’t know about it.” 

It was. And is. And six years 
later, the single best way to tap into 
the unbridled energy, fertile cre- 
ativity, and utter ridiculousness of 
Burning Man all year round, as 
well as meet other subversives who 
keep San Francisco, well, San Fran- 
cisco, is to visit Beale’s Laughing 
Squid [laughingsquid.org] cluster of 
Web sites, and to receive the Squid 


List, a calendar of events he mod- 


erates and posts four to eight times 

a day, five days a week. Now a Frisco 

fixture, the Squid List is a hot line to 

the absurd, with 7,200 subscribers. 
“Tr’s li 


contemporary version 
of a clique of French surrealists or 
writers who meet in a café, only we 
have a virtual meeting place,” says Har- 
rod Blank, who adds that the Squid 


List “turned out a crowd” for the Art 


Car Fest he coproduces. “Beale has 


really unified the counterculture.” 
Recently, the Squid has featured 
invitations to a few rounds of after- 
dark golf through the streets of San 
Francisco's North Beach; Change Your 
Mind Day, a free afternoon of medi- 
tation in a local park; and a three-hour 
bus tour, with unspecified stops for 
sketch comedy called Popcorn Anti 
Theater [www.popcornantitheater.coml. 


FULL BODY MAKEOVER Find your true avatar self at 
[threadnaught.net/troopers/troopers.htmll. 


Beale has used his pro bono Web 
sites to help artists win paying clients. 
He's also become an event producer 
himself, launching the Tentacle 
Sessions, a series of presentations by 
cryptozoologists, evangelists of fake 
religions, and sci-fi novelists. Next for 
Squid: regional links for those with a 
will to be weird in Houston, Min- 
neapolis, or.. Dayton. BRAD WIENERS 


FIGHT OF THE MONTH 
Sex onthe Run 


The Protagonist >» Gary Kremen, 
founder of Match.com and rightful 
owner of Sex.com. 


The Antagonist »» Stephen Michael 
Cohen, who stole the Sex.com domain 
from Kremen in 1995 and was recently 
ordered by a federal court to give it back, 
plus $65 million in damages. 


The Chase >» Cohen has allegedly fled 
the country; Kremen is offering a 
$50,000 reward for information on him. 


Whither Sex.com? »» The site, now 
back under Kremen's control, is being 
converted to a lucrative porn portal. New 
president Daron Babin sighs, “I look at 
how much money this site is now taking 
in and how much time passed while it 
was stolen, and | know how much 
money Kremen lost, and it's sad.” 


The Sightings »» Rumors in the adult- 
entertainment industry place Cohen in 
Tijuana, Mexico, where he reportedly 
lives large off money he transferred to 
offshore accounts. But so far, no Cohen. 


Pulp Fiction? »» Cohen's attorney 
recently sent a letter to Sex.com, 
claiming that two people were shot in 
Mexico while trying to apprehend Cohen 
for the $50,000 reward, and requesting 
that Kremen rescind the offer. 


Says Babin >» “This guy's just a down- 
tight crook. But eventually 
what goes around comes 
around.” JANELLE BROWN

FOR A’DAILY 
DOSE OF NET 
BUZZ, CHECK OUT 
YIL.COM/BUZZ 


75 


Illustration by Ulfa Puggaard 


hen this magazine launched five years ago, iY 

was employee No. 3. At the time, the mod- 

ern-day browser had just started to make its 

way into the mainstream. And almost 

overnight, the pundits and gurus would have 

us believe, surfing the Web—firing up 

Netscape Navigator or Internet Explorer 

and looking at a bunch of pages—became 

the most popular thing to do online. | 

‘Well, the pundits and gurus had it wrong: 

If that had been the real story, odds are you 

would have wandered off by now. Truth is; 

the most popular thing to do online five 

years ago remains the most popular thing 

to do online now. Simply said, the biggest 

online thing ever is e-mail—plain old joke- 

forwarding, boss-pestering, drop-a-note- 

to-a-friend e-mail. Even if you've never 

built a Web site, if you've never dipped your beak in the dot-com in~ 
vestment well, you are still absolutely at ground zero when it comes, 
to what's most important on the Net, to what people really do there, 

Surprised? No? Good. You're much smarter than a lot of people 
who claimed to know where wed be by now and how weld all be con-, 
nected—very connected, and in ways mighty strange to our postmil- 
lennial eyes. No one doubted that the Net would be huge. It's just that 
no one expected you to be looking at it the way you do now, which is 
largely the way you did then, my Web-roving, e-mailing friend. 

Five years ago, I sat in a lecture hall and listened to TV executives 
tell the assembled throng that we needed to talk about Net users as 

“viewers,” not “surfers,” because that's how things were going to be on 
the Net. Their logic was that those in the audience were geeks— 
people smart enough to build the technology that made the Net tick, 
but not smart enough to make it appealing. To do that, it had to be 
like TV, a proven crowd-pleaser in the nongeek world. i 

And what did the TV types think we wanted? Big “interactive”, 
entertainments created by the usual movie and television suspects; 
with an assist from people who were already producing soap opera-style 
sites like the Spot. Everything was going to be paid for by subscrip- 
tions and ads and people buying stuff they had to own right damn now, 
because they just saw it on Friends. i 

We surfers—sorry, viewers—were going to change, too. We were 
going to be avatars! We'd all have digital bodies and be in cyberspace, 
looking just like ourselves but...oh, you know, cyber. We were going 
to have information pushed straight to our desktops, no browsers, 
needed. Heck, maybe even no computers. Straight to our retinas with 
the daily news. Computers were going to vanish, or turn into TVs, 
or live in our refrigerators. Certainly we weren't going to be reading 
this on anything as 20th-century as paper or a boxy beige computer 
monitor. And we were all going to be globalized day-trading zil- 
lionaires (but you know how that daydream ends). 

So much for the visionaries. Fact is, our reality turned out to be 
both more ordinary and more extraordinary than predicted. Fact is, 
you're not an avatar reading this inside your refrigerator (nor, hap- 
pily, am 1). Fact is, almost all of us still use computers to get online, 
or use TVs that aren't too pleased to be pressed into computing serv 


78 Yahoo! Internet Life > September 2001 


7 
{ 
joe. Fact is, we tend to use our Net hookups to look up television list- 
ings and watch them on the same old TVs, leaving our computers 
to do what computers do better. Fact is, more of us are online and 
surfing the Web and e-mailing than ever before. 

| Humanity, not technology and not TV-style fabulousness, car- 
ried the day. As it turns out, the geeks were willing to rediscover their 
humanity alot faster than were the entertainment moguls, who out- 
smarted themselves about what it is that people want from their time 
‘not spent sleeping and eating. 

| Partof what we want is something more than entertainment. Few 
‘among us are above a little mindless couch-potato time now and 
then, but we are hardly, as a book once claimed, “amusing ourselves 
to death.” The true shape of the Net reminds us that there are places 
in our lives where mindless mass-market entertainment can't go: We 
like to talk amongst ourselves (where were e-mail and chat in the 
grand 1996 Net vision?). We like to share (was Napster even a fever-_ 
sh glimmer in the music industry's eye?). We like to mark a place for 
‘ourselves in the world (where, in all the cant about interactive 
‘shopping, was the nascent nation of online weblog diarists, pouring 


lout their souls one hyperlinked paragraph at a time?). 


| The 2001-era Net is a product of real people getting online, not 
a variety of next-generation TV. Against all odds and a lot of ven- 
ture-capitalist oddsmaking, the Net is still an individuals-thinking- 
for-themselves proposition. Ain’t nobody gonna tell you where to 
surf. Ain’t nobody gonna make you sit back and suck down “content” 

as if it were soda We killed those models dead. Because the Net is 
surfed click by click, every single person surfing shapes the Net in her 
‘own image, simply by visiting things that interest and appeal to her. 
“The soul of the Net is a human soul. And while the early adopters 
may wail that real people reduce their grand tech schemes to the 
unglamorous likes of e-mail, they should be grateful that they're not 
‘those TV executives; the Net, like common sense and folk wisdom 
‘and other basic good instincts, turned out to be beautifully cruel to 
the greedy, the arrogant, and the clue-deprived. ; 


| 


| The early adopters may also wail (correctly) that the AOLs and 
‘Microsofts and Yahoo!s of the world turned their pleasant country- 
side ramble into a chicken match on a corporate highway. And we hear 
that wail, but remind them (and us) that the idea of an “interstate sys- 
tem” is to allow heavy traffic to travel from place to place. So let’s give 
thanks to Yahoo! for paving the way; to Microsoft for a single (elf- 
enriching) standard; to AOL for getting 30 million people online. 

But as we ease on down a road increasingly owned by just a hand- 
ful of media powers, let's remind ourselves that it’s along the 
byways— fall of homegrown passions, labors of love, and let’s-share- 
‘my-obsession—thata more soulful Net still grows in ways that can’t be 
measured or monetized. If we come to treat the Net merely as a util- 
ity—a better phone book, a fresher weather report—we lose sight of 
tthe fact that we wrested it from people who had other plans in mind. 
| worry about forgetting that. But I worried about the TV peo- 
ple five years ago, and we beat them back. Let'us watch and wait. Let 
‘us roam ever more freely. The frontier is still open. The Internet has 
‘not always responded to the proddings of visionaries or of the peo- 
‘ple who would use it to make money, but it has unfailingly evolved 
to serve us—our needs, our desires, our basic human goals. The Net 
pias changed. We, thankfully, have not.

e-mail at the office. As they grew more comfortable with the medium, they joined online communi- 
ties, such as Yahoo! GeoCities [geocities.com] and Tripod Itripod.coml, where members create personal home 
pages and find people with common interests. | remember teasing a former colleague when she sub- 
scribed to a newsgroup about painted turtles, but where else could she find advice on how to get her cat 
to stop stalking her new pet? Obscure hobbies also helped drive the enormously popular eBay lebay.coml, 


the person-to-person auction site that’s emerged as one of the Web's largest de facto communities (though 


the party, The number of senior citizens who are online has grown from 8 percent in 1996 to 34 per- 
cent, as retirees find new ways to keep in touch with family, especially grandchildren. Web use among teenagers has in- 
rcent, since 1996, and the generation gap is shrinking. A casual survey

Whitman 


The president and CEO 
of eBay, the world's 


largest online trading 

community, explains why 
it takes a village to grow 
a metropolis 


In the community where you live, 
you end up being friends with peo- 
ple who like the same things you 
do—fly-fishing or art or whatever. The Net 
ties people together along a common area of 
interest, but without regard to geography or 
time. The fact that eBay was a whole new thing 
captivated people, It was like, “You couldn't do 
this before—this is really cool!” That's why a 
community grew up around it. When there's 
something new in town, people talk about it, 
and they talk about it fora long time. 
However, community for community's sake 
isa difficult business model. The reason eBay 
has worked is that itis anchored in buying and 
selling or, as we say at eBay, trading, People had 
a reason to be at the site, and then discovered 
folks of a like-minded interest. The core of 
that community is the Feedback Profile, which 
was a deliberate moye to ensure a feeling of 
trust and a sense of identity for u 


. People 


getan enormous kick out of the profiles. I love 
to see what people say about me. 

I've been asked, “Don't you hate that peo- 
ple criticize eBay on the message boards?” I 
don’t hate it at all. It’s our best form of cus- 
tomer research. Two years ago, Auction Watch 
began having pretty vibrant discussions about 
eBay on their site. I said to our team, “Get 
‘em back here and make sure we have as ro- 
bust a forum for people to complain about 
eBay on eBay.” 

The challenge forus is how to grow big and 
stay small, We're like a village growing into a 
city, and within that city are 
districts—the diamond dis- 
trict, garment district, flower 


can imagine something no one else 
has (sorry—an homage to the Valbuena 
Chimps is here already), you can cre- 


ate your own community.

NEL 


lt started out slowly, but the Net got up to 
speed with a broad range of financial services 


If you've used the Internet to manage your money, you know how far it has come 


in five years. “Online banking” was initially as much a misnomer as “mili 


intelligence,” because banks that purported to pay your bills electronically were 
often just mailing out paper checks. For investors, the Net was more of a toy 
thana tool: Research was sparse, and most stock quotes were delayed by 20 min- 
utes or more—meaning that small e-traders were always running behind Wall 
Street insiders. @) These days, online financial transactions move at warp speed. 
The major banks enable customers to transfer funds instantly. The task of ap 

plying for a credit card, or even a home-equity loan, takes as little as 60 sec 

onds—with an on-the-spot answer to boot. And with real-time stock quotes 


readily available across the Net, the little guys can act on the same market intelligence as 


the pros. (@)At the same time, obstacles imposed by legislators at the behest of brick- 


saction costs. Consumers 


and-mortar lobbies have limited the Net's ability to reduce trar 


may never fully reap the huge potential savings from buying insurance policies over the In 


ternet, for instance, because federal regulations now require insurers to offer coverage for a 
uniform price. Similarly, state franchise laws have frustrated the efforts of e-tailers such as 
CarsDirect.com to sell directly to consumers. ®) The good news is that personal finance sites 
can now help consumers see their entire fiscal picture. Account aggregation services consol- 
idate credit card bills with bank, brokerage, and mutual fund statements. And a new breed of 
companies, among them Financial Engines [financialengines.com] and Morningstar ClearFuture 
[cf.morningstar.coml, have developed sophisticated digital tools that offer specific advice on how 
to invest your assets to meet life goals. ®) Still, artificial intelligence goes only so far. With 


millions of pages of new content being added to the Net each year, many investors may con- 


clude that they need a real-life financial adviser to play the role of human portal. DEAN FOUST

Considering the tremendous rise 
and spectacular fall of the dot-com 
stock market “bubble,” few peo- 

ple are unaware of how deeply the popular~ 
ization of the Internet has affected financial 
culture. More Americans now own equities 
than at any time in our history, and such wide- 
spread participation seems to be a fact of 
modern life. For better or worse, this enthu- 
siasm was fueled by dot-com mania. 

Far less understood is the degree to which 
the Web has changed financial journalism. 
The rise of such sites as The Motley Fool [fool.coml 
and CBS MarketWatch Imarketwatch.coml quickly 
demonstrated how this very powerful medium, 
even in its nascent stage, could change dra- 
matically in response to user input. 

Conversely, in newspaper and television 
content, the user plays a relatively passive role, 
which scared some old-media faithful early in 
the game. I remember the two views that tele- 
vision people first held about the Net: that it 
lacked the resources to compete and should 
be ignored; and that it was a threat to what 
we do and should be crushed. 

Both those initial thoughts were wrong, Our 
experience at CNN.com is that trafficactually rises 
with developments on our news shows. Our 
Web sites, including CNNfn [ennfn.coml, are 
critically important on both a stand-alone ba- 
sis and as an adjunct to cable programming. 

Interestingly, the brokerage industry learned 
the same lesson that television newscasters 
did: The Web has simply augmented the pie, 
not redistributed the pieces. The day trader 
who acts on some impulse to buy and sell 
every three minutes—may she rest in peace— 
has been replaced by those of us in the mar- 


Lou 
Dobbs

after a stint at Space.com. 
Here, he gleans lessons from 
a chastened market and 
explains how the Net changed 
financial reporting 


ket in 401(k)s and pension funds. But the Net is still 
an efficient way to trade stocks. And by organizing 
financial reporting in a way that is most con- 
venient to the user, it has created a higher 
level of efficiency in the marketplace. 


tures such as off-hours trading keep in- 
vestors coming back; offering the first 
25 trades free attracts new customers. 
Perhaps just not as many as before.

George D. 


Before I became an editor, I was 

running a pathology lab in Los An- 

geles County. Tests were done by 

the thousands, and delays in passing infor- 

mation from the lab to a doctor interfered 

with a doctor's ability to treat patients. Being 

able to transmit information online over long 

distances, flawlessly and cheaply, was a won- 
derful advancement. 

Internet tools that help consumers man- 

age their own health —checking blood pres 


sure, monitoring diabetes—are here to stay. 


Within the next five years, physician-to- 


physician consultations online will become 
extremely common. More images will be 
transmitted, and more decisions made, with- 
out worrying about the fact that the best 
doctor for providing a second opinion is 
thousands of miles away. 

I think physicians will sort through pa- 
tient security concerns. And unless they know 
a patient personally, doctors will be conser- 


and more of them online, it's little sur- 
prise to see this site in the Top 10. Spe- 
cial emphasis on such conditions as 
Alzheimer's disease and osteoporo- 
sis makes HealthandAge a favorite of 
mature Web surfers.

Institute Research Lab, a modest little promotional site for the movie Twister, with some 
cute animated whirlwinds. Today, it’s retro; in 1996, this was state of the art. But at least 
Twister had a Web site—most films in 1996 didn’t Oacross all sectors, entertainment has 
proved to be one of the areas in which the Internet has had the most cultural impact. And 
a few landmark projects have become synonymous with key industry developments. Film 
had The Blair Witch Project, the ultralow-budget 1999 hit whose success was fueled by am- 


ateur sites, proving to studios that the Web was a promotional gold mine. Music had 


Napster Inapster.coml, the file-sharing program that launched scores of lawsuits, waking the record labels up to the pos- 


sibility of online distribution. Sports had fantasy leagues, which gave fans a good excuse to spend all day clicking through 


reams of statistics (e) But none of these things happened in a vacuum. The Blair Witch hype was fueled by movie ru- 


mor networks such as Ain't It Cool News [aintitcool.coml. The promise of increased bandwidth made Napster a real threat 


And who would have been crunching those sports stats without well-financed megahubs such as ESPN 


fespn.coml? © Amusingly enough, five years ago, many believed that the future of Web entertainment lay in original 


content. Today, the list of original-programming casualties 


goes on and on: DEN, Entertaindom, Pop, Pseudo. Napster 


succeeded because it could get us the songs we could also 


buy in real stores. The Blair Witch hype whetted our appetites 


for a movie playing in honest-to-God movie theaters. And 


fantasy leagues are exciting because they deal with real play- 


ers. Old media, it seems, still rule

The Net scared the hell out of peo-

Then they got involved in it to pro- 
tect their properties, Today, the major record 
labels have co-opted the Net, just as with any 
other technology. The public got a head 
| start on the industry, and then the industry 
cried foul. 
One of the most revolutionary things of 


the past five years was Al Teller’s online label, 
Atomic Pop. He took a lot of hits for fixing 
the price barometer. That's taboo in the in- 
dustry—you don’t mess with the standard re- 
tail price. Of course, Napster and MP3 as a 
format were also revolutionary because they 
went around the corporations. The five ma- 
jors control the record industry; three corpo- 
rations control the radio industry; and one 
controls music videos. The majors are now 
trying to co-opt online music because they 
know they can’t kill it.

Moore 


The CEO of LivePlanet, an 
interactive entertainment 
company he cofounded with 
Ben Affleck and Matt 
Damon, sees the Net as 
connective tissue between 
creators and their fans 


Lord of the Rings was such 

a big deal when it went 
online, but think about it: For The Grinch, 
they created a three-minute teaser, put iton 
a satellite, and told any TV distribution 
company they could haye it for free. And 
185 million people saw it within 18 hours, 
You're reaching way more people that way 
than through the Web, And let's face it, if 
youre Michael Bay and you want everybody 
to see how cool Pearl Harbor is, the last thing 
you want to do is make it a 30 MB down- 
loadable file, 

The communicative aspect of the Net is 
what's really important. And the best way to 
use the Net for entertainment is as the con- 
nective tissue between fans and participants. 
What'll be really interesting is when you have 
that communication on a mass level. Pro- 
ject Greenlight [LivePlanet’s online screen- 
writers contest] has created such a strong 
community that we're happy just to be along, 
for the ride, even though we started it. 

What's really changed this business 
is the tise of such sites as Ain't It Cool News 
[aintitcool.com] and Dark Horizons 
[darkhorizons.com]—if there's a test screen- 
ing, it’s going to be posted on the Web, Stu- 
dios tend to go inherently with what's worked 
before. But if you have 2 million people say- 
ing, “You know, I'd really like to see this,” 
then things change. American Pie [which 
Moore produced] was a great success be- 
cause it was created by a bunch of guys 
saying, “Y’know, it’s been a long time since 
we've seen a Porkys movie. We should make 
one.” Thatwill continue to hap- 
penas you have a more two-way 
relationship with your audience.

they were basically promotional; 
most of the clubs put up their yearbooks, 
their statistics, pictures, et cetera. In phase 
two, people realized they could now turn this 
into a revenue source by generating traffic 
and selling advertising around it

an Internet company. The Washington 
Capitals [washingtoncaps.coml sell more than 
60 percent of our tickets online. We launch 
all of our news first on the Web. For exam-

to buy season tickets but couldn't afford all 
of the payment up front. So we decided wed 
do a deal where they could pay it without in- 
months. We

The brief, checkered history of e-commerce can be divided into three distinct epochs: Before 


Boo (“Boo Who?”), After Boo (“Boohoo”), and the New Boo (“Boo Two’). Boo, of course, was Boo.com, 
the impossibly hip fashion e-tailer no one had heard of until it imploded very publicly in May 2000.9 Be- 


| fore Boo, hanging out a virtual shingle sounded cheap, easy, and smart. No overhead! No middlemen! Launch 


it, and they will come. And for a fleeting moment in 1999, it did seem possible. Amazon.com was 


already scaring the wits out of bookstores with its 1-Click ordering. But then the cost of warehouses, so 


phisticated software, and customer service began scaring the wits out of 


investors. And despite Boo's appeal to fashionistas, the takers for its vir- who sa te eipeople 
who say ernet 
improves their ability 


to shop: 34 


tual shopping trip were virtually none. Before long, everyone was hailing 


| Boo’s swift and spectacular demise as proof that Net shopping had been 
| astupendously bad idea.©) After Boo, a long list of retailers—eToys, Fur- 


niture.com, Pets.com, and PlanetRx.com, to name but a few 


disappeared 


faster than an XFL advertiser. The survivor nked their Super Bowl 


ads, installed toll-free phone numbers, cut off the freebies, mailed catalogs, merged 
with brick-and-mortar heavyweights...in short, began acting like conventional stores.

Macthe 


Stewart 


The chairwoman and 

chief executive of Martha 
Stewart Living Omnimedia 
talks about shopping sites 
that work—and what 
excites her about the Net 


1 don’t enjoy shopping that much. I 
would rather spend my time driving 
toa beautiful mountain hike than to 

astore, If] could efficiently find everything I 
need on the Internet and have it delivered 
safely to my home, | would be thrilled. 1 do 
buy books, dog toys, clothes, gardening things, 
stuff on eBay, (I also haye plenty of things to 
sell on eBay, but I haven't had time!) I think 
many people feel the same way 1 do. 

The e-commerce sites that failed spent too 
much money on the wrong things. Building a 
brand takes time, effort, extreme caution, and 
alot ofinvestment. You have to be trusted. You 
have to be needed. Most of those companies 
were none of those things. 

You shouldn't presume to know what cus- 
tomers want, as many sites do now, I don’t want 
toget'a “Dear Martha” e-mail message that sup- 
poses | want a pair of yellow shorts because I 
boughta pair of yellow shorts last year. You have 
to develop a relationship with your customers 
and let them ask you, “I need a taupe-colored 
cotton sun hat. Do you have one?” We're work- 
ing hard on the next generation of our site for 
this fall, so customers can havea better dialogue. 

What excites me about the Net? The reach! 
And the volume of information that’s available. 
Now we have to be conscientious editors. 
You have to know that if you go to a site you 
will get accurate information. 

I am very encouraged by people’s curios- 
ity and their willingness to experiment, to use 
the Net forsaving time, not wasting 
time. That's what I find exciting 
and what I really want to help with.

Want to trek the Himalayas? Schedule a family reunion and loop in all the far-flung branches? 
Book a car at the Houston airport, then get a free map and driving directions? No problem 
Within the past five years, Web users have become their own travel agents with surprisingly lit- 


tle fuss or confusion. Indeed, in the past two years alone, the use of traditional travel agencies 
has declined 29 percent. ®) Since the launch of both Travelocity.com and Expedia.com in 1996, 
the Net has emerged as a near-perfect medium for buying what is essentially a virtual product: 


ane-ticket and printable itinerary. Almost a third of all online transactions now take place on travel sites—the only Net sector 


to enjoy increased revenues in 2000, the year of the dot-com crash. ‘@)“Five years ago, Web surfers were hard-pressed to find 


lodging information online,” recalls Durant Imboden, author of Buying Travel Services on the Internet and the guide of About: 
Europe for Visitors [goeurope.about.coml. “Now every little two-star hotel and B&B has its own site. The result has been a 
vast increase in clutter.” ®) Travelers continue to cut through the clutter, becoming ever-smarter shoppers. We've 
witnessed an evolution in ticketing sites—Cheap Tickets 
Icheaptickets.com], Lowestfare.com, Priceline.com, Hotwire 
thotwire.coml, Orbitz [orbitz.coml—each variation more com- 
petitive than the last. Improved customer service, en- 
hanced cruise bookings, streaming destination videos, and 
extensive niche-travel programs are already here, “@) What's 
next? Jupiter Media Metrix predicts that online travel 
purchases will increase from $18 billion in 2000 to $63 
billion in 2006. The biggest players are focusing on bet- 
ter customization, more real-time wireless services, and 
easier ways to help you book dining and entertainment 
online. As your own virtual travel agent, you'll still be able 


to write your own ticket. SUSAN BRESLOW SARDONE

The travel editor for NBC's 


When travel first hit the Web, con- 
sumers were proud that they were 
able to do the proper clicks and fill 

in the right boxes—only to find out that they 

weren't necessarily getting the best deals. Now 
you have a whole new generation of people say- 
ing, “Wait a minute. If we can't browse the 

Internet like we browse the supermarket, then 

we don’t want to play.” So sites such as Trave- 

locity and Expedia are giving people better 
comparison-shopping opportunities. 
Ofcourse, when the airlines saw all these 
seats being sold by online agents, they thought 
they could make more money selling directly 
to the public. Hence Orbitz [orbitz.com], which 
just launched in June. The jury is still out on 


whether Orbitz is a good thing for consumers. 
Initially, it might come across with some re- 
ally good deals. And why not? It's owned by 
the airlines. So it could hit the ground run- 
ning, and in the process make some of these 
other companies go out of business. We have 
here the potential for a huge restraint-of- 
trade situation. 

No matter which site you use, ifyou want 
round-trip tickets, you can pretty well play 
the game on the Internet. But I'll give you 
an example of where it doesn’t work. 

The other day I needed to book a ticket 
from Milwaukee to Minneapolis, then from 
Minneapolis to L.A., L.A. to Toronto, and back 
to LA. I did iton the phone witha human be- 
ing; the fare was $407 for the entire itinerary. 
I then tried it on the Internet, using every sys- 
tem possible, The lowest 
fare was $1,600! Any travel 
site that can bridge that gap 
will have a competitive edge.

faster than television, faster than maybe even fire making. Fiv 
lion of us online worldwide. Now there are well over 400 million. 
| changed, too. In 1996, for every curious female checking out an AOL chatroom, there were nine 
guys waiting to pounce. The numbers are now either even or slightly female-heavy. Just 14 percent 
of public schoolrooms had Net access in ’96, as opposed to 77 percent in 2000. And since a 
reasonably Web-worthy computer cost about $2,000 (compared with around $800 today), a real 


economic barrier existed for many potential Netizens.

NEL L 


The Net has grown faster than any technology in the history of the world—faster than printing, 


which postulates that computers will essentially double 
in power every 18 months. Moore's Law is, according to 
its creator, likely to expire in around five to seven years, 
when the very physics of technology will be the barrier 
holding us back. (You can make a computer only so small 
before you can’t see it or use it.) The past five years rep- 
resent just over three iterations of Moore’s Law in action. 


We have at least three left to go. Wherever we are head

During the last five years a broader 
public actually got onto the Inter- 
net. People have more power over 

their own lives, and the power of large institu- 

tions is eroding. 

On the other hand, we may soon long for the 
golden days of the “Open Net.” Because five or 
10 years from now, everybody on the Net will 
have some form of ID. I assume there will still 
be sites where there is free interchange. But in 
most places online, you'll be registered, rated, cer- 
tified, and authenticated. It’s going to be a chal- 
lenge to fight for some of the freedoms we take 
for granted now. Privacy shouldn't be legislated 
by governments, but should be defined by indi- 
viduals who control their own data. 

I've been spending a lot of time recently in 
Central and Eastern Europe, where the Inter- 
net has a different flavor from here in the US. 
For example, I’m on the board ofa commercial 
company that uses the Net to help people find 
jobs, and to help employers find workers and 
train them. That's a unique and vital service 
there, and you feel like you're doing some- 
thing incredibly useful. 

Fundamentally, the Net makes the world a 
smaller place. When I went to Russia in 1989, I 
hadan MCI mail account which I didn’tuse much 
because there wasn’t anyone to write to. Then I 
started communicating with the people I met in 
Russia, and it made the trip more valuable because 
I could communicate with them after I got back. 
The Net's not going to make the Serbs love the 
Croats or solve all these political prob- 
lems, but it will encourage people to 
think more globally, which is great. 


Esther 


Dyson 


The Internet pioneer, one 
of its first governing 
officials, worries about 
online privacy. But she 
also sees the Net bridging 
global barriers

MEL Uf 


I knew the Net had come a long way when I blew the dust off my first book, Internet Games Directory (Que, 


1996), and noticed that its cover boasted “FTP Sites!” and “Hundreds of MUDs!” Those two acronyms 
stand for file transfer protocol and multi-user dungeons, but don’t worry if you've never heard of them. Before 1996, 
Net gaming was a niche pastime. Q Everything changed on August 30, 1996, when a little game called Quake 
hit retail shelves. The revolutionary—and controversial—3-D shooter became the hottest thing on the Net 
since porn, thanks to its heated 16-player action and its unprecedented community of players—who, when 
not playing Quake, swapped custom-made modifications. Ultima Online, which launched the following 
year, supported thousands of simultaneous players and spawned today’s so-called persistent worlds, which 
exist even after the user logs off. If it weren't for Ultima, EverQuest gamers would not have spent much of 
last year selling their characters on eBay (before the practice was banned). Spending countless hours in 


digital dungeons isn’t for everyone, and casual gaming has exploded on free multiplayer sites. Just about every 


classic card, board, casino, and tile game is now on the Web, as is nearly every conceivable T V-licensed game 
show (thanks, Regis). And gaming is no longer a boys-only club. Recent studies show that females make up 51 percent of 
today’s online players, as well as 43 percent of computer and console game players. © In 1999 the ill-fated Sega Dreamcast 
became the first video-game system to ship with a built-in modem. Sony, Microsoft, and Nintendo have since 
made a firm commitment 
to multiplayer gaming 
over a TV set. Indeed, 
Sony plans to incorporate 
AOL’ instant-messaging, 
chat, and e-mail capabili- 
ties into the PlayStation 2 


platform. Other trends in-

The concept of a virtual world was 
the thing that attracted me most to 
online gaming, From my early days, 
when I played Ultima and Wizardry, to the 
creation of today’s modern three-dimensional 


realms, seeing virtual worlds grow more and 


more realistic has been rewarding. 

When it comes to the Net’s impact on 
gaming, the first thing that usually jumps to 
mind are the actual games being played on- 
line, such as Quake. But in the larger scheme 
of things, the formation of gaming commu 
nities is more important. There are lots of peo- 
ple that spend more time online talking about 
gaming than actually playing games online. 

In five years, developers will be willing 
to sell games that absolutely require high- 
bandwidth connections. It won't bring a 
flood of new features that people haven't 
seen before, but it will make developers’ lives 
easier and let us concentrate more on the 


fundamentals of gaming. 


s will lead 


to interesting new styles of gaming. Always 


I think handheld wireless devic 


being connected to the Net will lead to bet- 
ter multiplexing between the real world and 
the online worlds you play in. 

Looking any further into the future is 
challenging. Coupling more bandwidth with 
ever-increasing processing power and greater 
penetration of video camera devices will 
probably lead to avatars that can accurately 
convey mappings of facial expressions and 
other body gestures. 

Still, | am more excited by the conven- 
tional aspects of the Internet. Hardly a day 
goes by that I don’t conduct at least adozen

better developers will result.
