BEST 


12/96 CERN researchers Tim 
Berners-Lee and Robert Cailliau 
create a system that links related 
information on Internet-connected 
computers. Grasping for metaphors, 
they describe it as a “web.” 


2/93 Led by Marc Andreessen, a 
team of students from the University 
of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign 
builds Mosaic, a program that puts 
a graphical face on the previously 
text-only Web. 


12/94 Fiash-forward: Andreessen 
releases Netscape 1.0, a better, faster, 
stronger browser that eclipses 
Mosaic. Surfers make the switch. 


2/94 Stanford grad students David 
Filo and Jerry Yang cobble together 

an online list of a few dozen of their 

favorite Web sites. “Jerry's Guide to 

the World Wide Web” soon becomes 

better known as Yahoo! 


4/95 Talk amongst yourselves. AOL, 
Prodigy, and CompuServe—the three 
largest online services—let users 
send and receive Internet mail. 


8/95 i 
rocket i mg28 comin Bee | 3 0) N ET EVE N TS eee 
days, setting off a four-year dot-com 


IPO frenzy that takes the Nasdaq past 
5,000 before it cools off... way off. 


1/96 President Clinton mentions the 


word /nfernef for the first time in a 

State of the Union address, 

announcing a plan fo connect all of 

America's classrooms within four years. 

5/96 Look at the ugly mug on that 

crook...and download it. The FBI nabs 

armed robber Leslie Rogge after pres- 

sure generated by its nascent Web site, | 
the 10 Most Wanted Fugitives, leads 

him to surrender in Guatemala. 

9/96 Napster is still three years | 
away when online visionary David | 
Bowie becomes the first major record- | 
ing artist to release a single online. 

12/96 Don't hang up! AOL switches 

from per-minute charges to all-you- 

can-surf subscriptions. Six million 

happy chatters stay online for hours, 

unexpectedly swamping AOL's dial-in 

lines. (See also “Worst Moments.") 


5/97 Checkmate! Some 20,000 
people in 120 countries go online to 
watch Garry Kasparoy, considered by 
many to be the best chess player in 
history, lose to IBM's powerful 
supercomputer Deep Blue. 


6/97 Netizens and the First Amend- 
ment win a major battle when the 


Supreme Court rules against the 
draconian Communications Deceno 
Act. The decision confirms the Netis, in 
fact, a mass medium like TV and radio. 


7/97 Attention, Earthlings! The 
Mars Pathfinder probe lands and 
sends back photos. Online viewers 
ogle them 47 million times in one day. 


1/98 A 30-year-old computer novice 
named Matt Drudge breaks the 
Monica Lewinsky story online. Within 
days, the rumor-filled Drudge Report 
becomes must-read maierial. 


11/98 President Clinton signs the 
Digital Millennium Copyright Act, up- 
dating outmoded laws for the Net Age. 


11/98 aoL begins an acquisitive 
onslaught, gobbling up Netscape for 
$4.2 billion and positioning itself as a 
major media player. Industry watchers 
wonder what will come next. 


2/99 Victoria's Secret scores a lace- 
trimmed touchdown when ifs online 
fashion show, which had been 
advertised during Super Bowl XXXIIl, 
sends 1.5 million drooling sports fans 
scrambling for their computers. 


2/99 No pens, no fellers. The Net's 
first full-service bank, the apily named 
First Internet Bank of Indiana, sets up 
shop and begins accepting deposits. 


5/99 Onetime Northeastern 
University student Shawn Fanning 
(nicknamed “Napster’) puts the 
finishing touches on his little 
peer-to-peer file-sharing system 
and shows a few friends how they 
can use it to swap music files. 


7/99 With a $140 million box-office 
gross, The Blair Witch Project proves 
that a well-executed Web site can 
generate more buzz than all the 
advertising strategies of a roomful of 
Armani-clad studio execs. 


12/99 Sir Paul McCariney goes 
back to where he once belonged when 
his online concert, live from Liverpool's 
Cavern Club (capacity: 300), attracts 

3 million people to MCY.com. 


1/00 At12:01 am. on January 1, 
someone successfully withdraws 
money from an ATM in New Zealand, 
and we know that after all the fear- 
mongering (and consulfing fees), the 
Net has survived Y2K just fine. 


1/GG Ifthe Netis infinite, why does it 
feel so crowded? Because the number 
of Web pages has passed the billion 
mark, making search engines among 
the Net's most popular sites. 


1/60 Paying more than $2 million 


per 30-second ad fo show sock 
puppets and flying gerbils, 17 =) 


dot-coms, including Pets.com and 
Monster.com, account for a large 
chunk of ABC's $130 million 
Super Bowl XXXIV take. 


1/GG New media trumps old as AOL 
merges with Time Warner to the tune 
of $160 billion. The International 
Federation of Journalists warns that the 
deal could threaten democracy and 
freedom of speech. 


3/UU Angry Kid, a Claymation short 
released by AtomFilms (now called 
AtomShockwave), becomes the first 
Net-based movie fo be nominated for 
an award at the Cannes Film Festival. 


8/6 A coalition of instant- 
messaging providers, including AT&T, 
Microsoft, and Yahoo!, agrees on a 
standard to let users send IMs to one 
another regardless of the platform 
there using. AOL doesn't cooperate. 


L As Brokaw, Jennings, and 
Rather botch election-night results, 
their underlings rush to Florida state 
government Web sites to get more 
accurate vote counts from precincts 
around the Sunshine State. 


/GG Online holiday shopping 
passes the $12 billion mark, or 164 
percent of 1999's total of $73 billion, 
suggesting that despite countless 
dot-com disasters, the world is 
embracing Net shopping. 


2/01 Napster cranks it up to 11, 
teaching its zenith as users download 
2.79 billion files during the month. 
On an average day, a remarkable 
1.57 million users manage to 

log on simultaneously. 


9/88 College student Dave Rhodes 
allegedly sends the first "Make Money 
Fast” e-mail, claiming you can turn $5 
into $50,000. Rhodes says the e-mails 
made him more than $400,000. 


11/88 Twenty-three-year-old grad 
student Robert Mortis releases a 
“worm virus’ that crashes more than 
6,000 computers on Arpanet and 
Milnet, government-owned 
predecessors to the Internet. 


7/95 Fake research findings by 
Carnegie Mellon undergraduate 
Martin Rimm are picked up by Time 
magazine, which then runs a cover 
story on the ubiquity of pornography 
online. It later emerges that Rimm's 
statistics were fabricated. 


12/96 Paramount tersely demands 
that Star Trek fan-site owners 
remove various elements of the 
show (especially pictures) from the 
Net within 10 days, or else. The 

fans, naturally, rebel. 


f Immediately after AOL 
migrates to its flat-rate monthly plan, 
the ensuing gridlock spurs 36 state 
attorneys general to demand cash 
refunds for unhappy members who 
can't get online. 


{97 


3/9 Thirty-nine Heaven's Gate cult 
members commit mass suicide 

in a California mansion. The group 
conducted most of its membership 
recruiting on the Web, so the Net 
takes the rap for this tragedy. 


2/97 When the Navy calls AOL 
to ask if a profile including the word 
gay belongs to Chief Petty Officer 
Tim McVeigh (not the Oklahoma City 
bomber), AOL promptly tells— 
without McVeigh's consent. This 
leads to his dismissal. 


5/98 Users across the Web 
heroically unite—to help Howard 
Stern regular Hank the Angry Drunken 
Dwarf win People magazine's Most 
Beautiful People Poll online. 


6/98 Following the first “live Internet 
birth," happy cybermom Elizabeth Ann 
Oliver is jailed for check fraud. 


9/98 The Starr Report, containing 
titillating details of President Clinton's 
escapades, is released online. 


8/99 Questionable celebrity vanity 
hits an all-time low when questionable 
celebrity Carnie Wilson Webcasts 
her own gastric bypass surgery. 


99 The founders of the failed 
entertainment site DEN, which 
blew through $65 million in two 
years, are accused of sexual and 
financial crimes. The case is 
settled out of court. 


4/06 Having peaked in early 
March, the tech-heavy Nasdaq 
collapses, dragging most of the so- 


called New Economy with it. 


4/66 Fearing for their CD sales, 
Metallica and Dr. Dre sue Napster for 
copyright violation, bolstering a suit 
filed months earlier by the Recording 
Industry Association of America. 


¢/ UL After losing $830 million, 
online grocer Webvan—one of the 
most heralded e-commerce sites of 
the dot-com era—closes its doors 
and lays off its 2,000 workers.

EIGHT NOTED WRITERS ON A DEFINING NET MOMENT 


WHEN WAS YOUR MOMENT? 
DO YOU REMEMBER? HAS 
ANYONE EVER ASKED? 
Generations before ours witnessed the birth 
of their own media, recounted in stories of 
listening raptly to radio broadcasts from Ebbets Field, or of watching 
Uncle Miltie mugging on the Philco in the parlor. But we live in an 
altogether different age, one in which we've not only witnessed the 
birth of a medium but have also been invited to play an active role in 
its upbringing, It’s this participation that enlivens every hour we spend 
online, whether we're after the patent practicality of 1-Click shopping 
or the potent absurdity of All Your Base. 
In the pages that follow, eight of the most thoughtful writers of 
our time revisit their Net moment—the instant in which a two- 
dimensional medium became a truly great notion. 


www.yil.com September 2001 129 


What then happened was that the whole world passed me 
by. I was now the 50-year-old skier, who took up the sport at 
30, practicing my little turns diligently, even pridefully, only to 
notice one day that every human being aged three to 30 was 
gliding past me down the hill at 50 miles per hour doing en- 
trechats and arabesques and, if their eyes happened to fall on 
me, no doubt wondering that an old cripple could still manage 
to get down the mountain. 

That became the problem: to worry about all the things that 
the Internet holds out to you that you have not accumulated the 
knowledge to know about, let alone the skills to bring up. But never 
mind the fretfulness to which one is consigned on learning that 
there are green pastures out there that one will not likely ever har- 
vest. The magical moments continue, even if encountered by sheer 
chance and even if they lead to frustration. Here is mine. 

I wanted the keyboard music for a Bach concerto and dis- 
covered that I had to write to a store in London for it. I was as- 
tonished to get a bill for $40. (Itis, after all, only about 16 pages.) 
How can that be, given that there are no royalties to pay? A year 
later it was a Scarlatti sonata. I wanted the music for K.208, which 
I had once had and had lost. 


By William F. Buckley Jr. 


he great moment for everyone on whom the light 
of the Internet has shone has to have been read- 
ing on your screen what the other person said in 
reply to what you sent over, fingers trembling with 
excitement, just moments earlier. That was What- 
hath-God-wrought time, and I tore up the stair- 
case to announce to my wife that the revolution had come. I was 
so deranged as to think to myself that Lenin was finally vindi- 
cated after having said in effect that he needed only electricity to 
give him the world! The Internet wasn’t exactly the advent of 


electricity, but it is a child of electricity, and the afternoon when 


I completed my first round-trip in cyberspace, there was simply 
no doubting that I had the world by the tail 

That was my initial experience, and the years rolled by al- 
lowing me the conceit that I was among the discoverers of 
the computer world—not quite Thomas Edison or Bill Gates, 
but a prime apostle. There are 100,000 homes that have got 
hold of the new television mechanisms, such as TiVo and Ul- 
timateT V, that permit you to handle television as though an 
invisible librarian were working for you full-time, pulling out 
for you anything you wanted, mysteriously available. It is pre- 
dicted that by 2006, 10.2 million homes will have such devices, 
but those who came in early will continue to feast on the kind 
of satisfaction I had from coming in on the Internet ahead of 
a few other people in my circle. 


Why not try the Internet? A wild thought for me indeed. 

How (for an amateur) to begin? Circling around the name 
Scarlatti, | came upon The Classical Archives: Domenico Scarlatti 
Isunsite.univie.ac.at/classmidi/scarlatt.htmil. I scrolled down and was 
knocked over to find a screenful of K. citations, beginning with 
001 and stretching down to 555. I moved the cursor to K.208 


and, three seconds later, the music of that sublime piece of mu- 


sic came in over the speakers, a lovely rendition by John Sankey, 


who does the entire lot for you: all 555 of the Scarlatti sonatas. 


But that was serendipity. It was the sheet music I was after, 


and that search presented one of those little Internet problems 


that can be conclusive if you don’t have the patience and the re- 


sources to overcome them. How to get the sheet music for the A 
Major K.208 Sonata? 

Well, there is a seductive come-on. On the site, a button reads, 
Download selected free scores/sheet music. You click on it, and...nothing 
happens. Right under it you find, For sheet music: Get Adobe Acrobat 


Reader. Install to view them. 


I won't go on. In this case the instructions become techni- 


and 
are therefore incompetent to understand non-English. For 
some cyberlanguage there is no adequate dictionary. I end up 


cal beyond the capacity of those of us who write English 


empty-handed. The London music store lives on. 


But I know I would be a fool to complain about a medium 


that right now—right here! right this moment!—will play you 
any of the 555 Scarlatti sonatas! The Internet, for all its vexations, 


is heaven on earth, and the epiphanies 


are waiting for you, every day.

TIMES FIVE 
By Zadie Smith 


ecause the truth of it is, the medium doesn’t trans- 
form your world, not entirely. We do much of the 
same things, but we do them, yeah, faster, yeah, si- 
multaneously, yeah, in a different space. But there 
isn’t one epiphanic moment—at least, there wasn't 
for me. So here are the highlights. 

The time that was the first time. It was October 1994. I was 18. 


I was sitting in an underground college bunker named after the 
man who started the whole shebang, really started it: Alan Turing. 
I was having trouble double-spacing paragraphs on a Word doc- 
ument. My computer experience to date had been limited to a 
1989 Amstrad and a Canon Star Writer—the former, a game con- 
sole; the latter, a typewriter with aspirations. So, I'm sitting un- 
derground, struggling with my document. It is very late at night. 
I share the dark room with people I would not acknowledge in 
daylight. Nerds to the right, to the left. And they hate me, too, with 
my recreational drug use and my ethnic trousers and my inability 
to paginate. Time passes. Soon it will be 2 A.M. I have the feeling 


these guys are waiting me out—they want to see me come and beg 


for their help. Finally, humbled, I approach a tall, thin boy who is 


ring a fedora and has no friends. A clever man, no doubt, but 


an anemic one, with sallow skin and a strong smell. I ask him if he 
can help me. He is overjoyed. He leaves the game he is playing 
(he's 


volving 3-D hallways) and comes to my PC and leans over me. 


stalking someone around a medieval castle full of slowly re- 


In a second he has solved my problem. Ten minutes later he’s 
changed the font, realigned the paragraphs, inserted page num- 
bers, explained footnotes, and spell-checked the whole thing. | am 
almost weeping with gratitude. He has thrown off his usual crip- 
pling shyness. He's cracking jokes. Hes patronizing me. And then 


he says, 


“Have you activated your electronic mail account yet?” To 
me this sounds like “Have you considered the possibility of hu- 
man bipolar molecular transportation?” 

For halfan hour, he is infinitely patient. Here is the in-box with 
its black and white flashing bars, with its x’s and y's, with a folder 
to save your letters and a folder to throw them away. He keeps say- 
ing, “Imagine it’s just like your real desktop, your real office,” smil- 
ing at me, at the moron, who has to be spoon-fed the interface 
through imprecise, childish analogy, needing something solid 
and familiar to rest upon, like a crutch. 

So. In Turing's room that night I got a brief presentiment of 
what was to come. Blessed are the people you thought were los- 
ers, for they inherited the earth while you were busy admiring your 


own muscle definition and queuing for the new Prince album. 
The time | saw naked people. Like everybody, as soon as | 


was familiarized with the Internet, I searched round, seeing if 


I could find some naked people on it. And I could. And back then 
they didn’t want your money. Not really. They just wanted you to 


be impressed with them and the ingenious way in which they used 
this spanking new medium to increase the quantity of naked peo- 
ple it is possible to see in the world. Boy, did they succeed. For me, 
the first time was a mixture of Well, this is simple and quick and disease- 
free and cheering and, on the other hand, There might be nothing more 
lonely and more degrading to the spirit than this. Ah, the Web! There isn’t 
a thing in this world it can’t give to you, quick and flat and shiny 
and in full Technicolor. Except love, maybe. 

The time | found out | was not hot. Every culture dreams up 
its oracle of Delphi. You ask a question, you get the hard truth. 
Along with whydoihavetodie.com and isthereagod.com and 
howdoesonedogoodinthisworld.com, whether we like it or not, 
amihotornot.com is a pretty fundamental question. To cut short 
along and, frankly, upsetting story: I am not. Hot. I ama 37. Yes. 
And 10,976 people said so. 

The time | “met someone over the Internet.” | met someone 
over the Internet, once. And he was nota loon ora loser ora freak 
ora maniac. He was an angel. And we had a romance that would 
have been impossible even 10 years before. He saw me in a bar, he 
wrote about me ina Web piece. I found it (yeah, ego-surfing; I’ve 
stopped now), I mailed him. He mailed me back. I mailed him 
back (that carried on for a while). Then he messaged me, and I 
messaged him. Then we switched on the voice thing, and typed 
half a sentence, spoke the rest of it. About a month later, I put 
my face in a scanner and sentit to him. He took a picture of him- 
self with a digital camera and sentit back. I scanned my hands, my 
arms, my chest, and close-ups of my freckles. He played songs on 
guitars and recorded them and put them at secret addresses on the 
Web, and I went and listened to them, and hummed along like he 
was in the room. We met, often, in different cities. For nine months 
we had one hell of an affair. And then, when it ended, the bad news 


went both ways via retracted e-mail returned scans, voice-en- 


abler disabled, Messenger made invisible. I'm here but you don’t 
know I’m here. Same sadness as ever; different medium 

The time it really depressed the hell out of me. Losing e-mails. 
Either make the in-boxes bigger or...something. Because what we've 


got at the moment is a generation's correspondence going down 


the virtual toilet once every six months whether they like it or 
not. Nothing for the archives, nothing for their kids; no saved 
love letters, no histories, no memoirs. I lost 80 percent of my in- 
box in a stupid blunder—my own fault—and I don’t know that 
I have ever gotten over it. In fact, there’s your epiphany. When 
I lost my mails, the old Luddite in me thought, Well, this newfan- 
gled Internet business is as permanent as the word and as ephemeral as the word. 
By which I mean, consider this fact: Of novels published in the 
19th century, probably 98 percent are now out of print. By which 
I mean, you can have an explosion in the availability of the word, 
but the medium, by itself, is not enough. There has to be the 
will to preserve, too. And the determination to create good “con- 
tent,” above and beyond the ease of the medium (penny dread- 
fuls, as the cheap Victorian novels were called, got their name for

131 


a reason). Sooner or later the way the words travel to you ceases 
to thrill (think of the phone!). And all 


you're left with is what they mean.

INFINITE 
LOOP 


By Timothy Ferris 


ears ago, when Net surfing meant typing Unix 
commands at a%> prompt, I foresaw the online 
revolution. Given a graphic interface, I realized, 
the Internet would change the world. 


That was unfortunate—for two reasons. 


First, my crystal ball made me no money: It’s 
one thing to predict the petroleum revolution, quite another to 


realize that the man to back is an obscure, tight-lipped accountant 
named John D. Rockefeller. Second, I neglected to publish my 
speculations (I've never been able to find a literary voice suit- 
able for futurism) and thus missed out on the booby prize of at 
least becoming an Internet guru, with a little think tank, maybe, 
and a clutch of acolytes in eyeglasses. 

Thanks to my useless prescience, I was surprised neither when 
e-mail became ubiquitous nor when library catalogs, books, scientific 
journals, and the paintings in art museums went online. I'd seen all 
that coming. There was plenty I hadn’t expected—spam, porn, 
Pets.com—but in general I viewed the Internet boom with an inti- 
mate yet bereft bemusement. I felt like the cop who had married and 
been divorced from Marilyn Monroe while she was still a nobody. 

The main thing I failed to foresee was the Web's eerie efficiency. 
I hadn’t appreciated the extent to which it would function, not as 
a passive tool (if there is such a thing), but as an active, living or- 
ganism that climbs into your life and takes up residence there 

The first inkling of this came a few years ago, when I was writ- 


ing captions for a book of Hubble Space Telescope photographs. 


On my desk was a foot-tall stack of Hubble press releases, sorted 
by date, in which to check specific information about each im- 
age. But it didn’t take long to realize that finding the answers on 
faster than 


the Web—even with a 4,800-baud modem!—was 
pulling them out of the papers that lay within arm’s reach. 

It's often more efficient to find something on the Web than in 
the books in my own library. Since I wrote some of those books, the 


process can get spooky. A few weeks ago I wz 


searching the Inter- 


net fora suitable map of the Milky Way galaxy: After discarding sev- 


eral, I found one that looked just right. The credit line said it came 


from a book called Galaxies. The trouble is that I wrote Galaxies, and 


had designed the map. This wasn't research; it was academic onanism. 
The other day I was fact-checking the manuscript of my new 


book, Seeing in the Dark, and arrived at a sentence beginning with 


the words, “When the great comet of 1680 was moving like a 


ghost ship across the night sky, the theologian Christopher 


Ness warned that it was ‘a Sign from Heaven [that] signifies 


Drought, and portends War.” Wanting to verify the citation for 


the quote and the spelling of Nes 
1680 Christopher Ne 


’s name, I typed great comet 


into a search engine and hit Return. The first 


item to appear read, “..the great comet of 1680, moving like a 
ghost ship across the night sky.” 
I stared at it in nauseating confusion. Had I become some 
sort of inadvertent Internet plagiarist? No, what had happened was 
that in 1997 I'd published a piece in The New Yorker as a warm-up 
for the book, and it included the Ness quotation and my own “ghost 


ip” phrase. Somebody posted the article on a Web site, and now 


the Net had flung it back in my face. The weird thing is that the 
sentence in question had been edited out of the article before it 
was published. Evidently I was reading a bootleg of my own copy. 

Long ago, I wrote notes for a fiction piece about a future in 
which previously inanimate objects have all acquired a degree of 
digital intelligence: Your house gossips with your neighbors’ houses, 
your television watches what it wants, your doorknob talks back 
to you like a character in Disney’s Alice in Wonderland. Now some 


of that is coming true, although in different ways. My computer 


talks to other computers, unbidden, for reasons it doesn't bother 
to tell me about. The television set records programs I've never 
heard of, of its own volition. And as I write this, the flock of wild 


parrots that lives here on San Francisco's Telegraph Hill is ca- 


vorting in the tree outside my window, antic as a red-and-green- 
garbed motorcycle gang. Among them I can spot, | think, friendly 
Kristine and her mate, Sam. How do | know their names? Be- 
cause they have their own Web site, The Wild Parrots of San Fran- 
cisco’s Telegraph Hill [www.wildparrots.coml. 

Lying awake at night I ask myself; What now am I failing 
to foresee?

FAITH IN 
COMPLEXITY 
By Ha Jin 


espite being a regular Internet user, I've often had 
misgivings about the medium’ reliability and cred- 
ibility. On the Net anyone can give his or her two 
cents’ worth, and there is as much noise as there 


are voices. 


. At least I used to think that way. Be- 
sides, once I started browsing, I couldn’t stop 
for hours on end. Often I felt I had wasted time. 

Words on my computer screen looked suspicious to me be- 
cause they appeared without a clearly identified source and dis- 
appeared without a trace. On the Net, I realized, many people 
wrote under pen names—as if they all were trying to hide their 
faces. Asa result, I rarely viewed the Internet as a reliable source. 

That all changed recently. Last April, the EP-3 spy plane ac- 
cident was headline news in both the U.S. and China. The Amer- 
ican media presented the Chinese pilot Wang Wei, who was killed 
in the collision, as a mere hot-dog pilot. He appeared on TV like 
a clown, smiling foolishly and standing with his upper body above 
the opened canopy of an F-8 interceptor. The Chinese media, 
however, depicted him much differently. He became a national 
hero, who had been not only an ace pilot but also a considerate 
husband, an erudite man, an accomplished artist, a good chef, a 
skilled tailor, a singer, a computer wiz, and no doubt an expert 
in the fine art of ikebana (flower arranging) 

On American TV 
him. 


senator smirked and essentially said about 


Well, if you want to perform those stunts, you've got to have 
the skills. Otherwise you put others’ lives in danger and get yourself 
killed.” By contrast, the Chinese government, in addition to issuing 
Wang high honors, named him Guardian of Territorial Airspace and 
Waters. A song, “Brave Eagle,” was officially composed in his mem- 


ory. His bedridden wife was shipped to Beijing so that the national 
leaders could visit her in the limelight. Schools in the capital offered 
to take care of his baby son’s education before college. His hometown 
was making a towering marble statue of him. The whole thing had 
gone so out of proportion that people joked in private that Wang 
Wei must have been saved the minute he ejected, but the govern- 
ment had hidden him away so as to create a big hero. 

This di 


me, as I sensed that this guy’s life must have been distorted. Peo- 


repancy in the American and Chinese media puzzled 


ple often talk about cultural conflicts and clashes that cause mis- 


understandings, but I don’t buy any of that. There are facts and 


truths beyond cultural interpretations and justifications. They are 


not clear simply because some people have concealed them from 


us. What is the truth in this case, then? A series of Web sites, in 
English as well as Chinese, pointed me toward the facts. Wang 
Wei and his fellow pilots had been pressured into a tragic situa- 


tion: They were ordered to stop the American spy planes without 


firing at them. This kind of tragic dimension in an individual’s 
death was completely absent from the news reports most of us 
were exposed to. But I found it on the Web, a medium often mis- 
trusted, yet here, a place where multiple voices offered the truth 
in all of its complexity. 


Some articles on the Net purpose 


y highlighted the holes in 
the official reports. Some convincingly analyzed the psychology of 


the authori Some, using the same evidence given by the me- 
dia, offered an entirely original interpretation. Some revealed facts 
that had been suppressed and neglected. 

The discovery amazed me, as if suddenly I had found access 


to a roomful of cl 


ified files. This experience changed my feel- 


ing about the Internet. I was impressed by the honest, relentle 


voices on the Web working to unravel the official stories. The Net 
is indeed indispensable if we look for truth. Its power lies in its 
ability to shatter authorities’ manipulation of information. For this 


alone I will go on browsing.

A BIDDERIN 
PLATO’S CAVE 
By Russell Banks 


hen it came to the Internet, I was for years 
a troglodyte, which is defined by The Amer- 
ican Heritage Dictionary of the English Language 
as “a member of a fabulous or prehis- 


toric race of people that lived in caves, 


dens, or holes.” To me, a computer was a 
fancy typewriter, and the Internet was a way to mail letters called 
e-mail quickly and cheaply. Beyond that I didn’t want to know. 
I'ma novelist and screenwriter and had become comfortable over 
the years with software such as WordPerfect and Final Draft, but 
only so long as it came to me ona floppy disk that I could punch 


www.yil.com > September 2001 


133 


manually into my computer. The idea of downloading software 
directly from the Internet with the click of a mouse seemed too 


close to Ben Franklin’s experiment with the kite and key—I might 


get struck by lightning. My wife, long an Internet adept, calls it 
the blue flash syndrome. 

That was little over a year ago, and much has changed. 
Nowadays I think of myself as a “recovering troglodyte.” The “real” 


and electronic worlds have merged and become one for me. It’s 


as if I’ve taken up residence in Plato's cave. 


How did the concrete, physical, hands-on world of such ob- 


jects as printed manuscripts, brick-and-mortar libraries, CD- 
ROMs, and floppy disks become one with something as utterly 
abstract as electronic bits of information moving practically at 
the speed of light through my telephone line onto my computer 
screen? It happened, as they say, one night. 

After an extra glass or two of wine at dinner, I logged on to 
AOL to check my e-mail. No mail that evening, Bored and mildly 


disappointed, I clicked a banner ad for eBay, I remembered that 
a friend had been buying and selling antique furniture on eBay for 


several years and had made in that time twice her husband's salary, 
which, when I thought about it, was a little like winning at black- 


jack in Vegas, which is to say, cool, 


I clicked onto one of the categories of goods whose value I 


could reasons 


bly determine, I don’t know much about antique ar- 


moires, but as an American boy of the 1950s, I do know old cz 


Ss, 


and in minutes I wa 


checking out Lincolns of the 1970s, Then, 
before I could stop myself, I was bidding on a silver and black 
79 Lincoln Versailles. It seemed to be in perfect condition. Lo- 
cated in Athens, Georgia, it had no rust, new tires, rolled and 


pleated silver-gray leather upholstery, a trunk large enough to stash 


three bodies, and an ashtray that could hold half-a-dozen cigar 


butts. I decided that, if necessary, the Lincoln could be resold in 


upstate New York (mainly because of the absence of rust on it) 


for between $4,000 and $4,500. I bid $3,900. As it happened, I 


y into the auction a mere two hours before it was 


had poked my 


scheduled to close, and by night's end I owned, or had “won,” as 


eBay insists, a 1979 Lincoln Versailles sitting ina garage in Athens, 
Georgia, 1,100 miles from my garage in Keene, New York. My 
wife had gone to bed, but I knew her first question would be, “So 
how're you going to get it home, smart guy?” 


This was 


a problem for the Internet. I went clicking for 
companies that transport cars and within minutes had located 
an outfit in Gainesville, Florida, that specialized in transporting 
vehicles. For another $300, I was able to have my Lincoln de- 


livered to my door; when it rolled off the truck and the engine 


roared to life, then settled into a chuckling rumble, I knew that 
my life had changed. The car is what we used to call “cherry,” and 
when I drive it to town for the paper or over to Lake Placid to the 
movies, | feel like Tony Soprano, Within weeks, I had eBayed my 
way to an antique Rolex watch for $1,100, which, after having it 
appraised, I insured for three times as much. I bought a Bianchi 
racing bike in perfect condition, the complete, 22-volume, 2000 
edition of the Oxford English Dictionary, still in sealed cartons, for 


half the retail cost, the numbered Riverside edition of the com- 
plete Hawthorne, and a first edition of The Scarlet Letter. 
All right, I've collected early editions of Hawthorne for 


years, and I know dictionaries; also, I’m a longtime student 


and rider of French and Italian road bikes, I'd recently read 


an excellent piece on antique Rolexes in The New Yorker, and 


I’ve been checking out 1970s American luxury cars for years. 


So it wasn’t all luck. Still, my initiating ramp onto the Inter- 


net was the ramp to the mall, as it were, specifically the eBay 


mall, where the thrills of shopping and gambling meet. And 


since I didn’t get burnt there—no blue flash, and I made 


money~—I was willing and even eager to move on to the next 


ramp, and the next, and the next, leading eventually to my 


present wholehearted embrace of the Net, where, as in Plato's 


cave, metaphysical differences between the abstract and the 


concrete dissolve, and the ideal world 


becomes real.

THE ELECTRIC 
RUSH 
By Pico Iyer 


very time I use e-mail, I wonder and wonder again 
about the instant, rushing closeness it encourages 


and even creates. More intimate than a phone call, 


more urgent than a letter (and much more uncen- 


sored, and unnuanced), e-mail has exploded some 
friendships I've cherished for years and propelled 


me dangerously close to people I've never met. Suddenly—overnight, 


it seems, or quicker—strangers are under my skin and inside my 


soul, whispering things to me they'd never say in person, and 


getting my own strange whispers back. 


I first noticed how e-mail was creating a new kind of rela- 


tionship after I'd been using it for four years. About 10 years 


134 Yahoo! Internet Life > September 2001 


ago, I wrote a piece half-defending a young male novelist whose 
books people found offensive, even obscene—by placing him 
next to a young female novelist who seemed to me, in her 
subtle and emotional way, no less violent. Men aren’t the only 
aggressors in life, I'd meant to suggest; they're just the crudest 
and loudest kind. 

In May of last year, quite by chance, I found myself partici- 
pating in a reading in New York, and there, by my side, was the 
woman, unmet, whom Id used as a straw man (or woman) in my 
argument of nine years before. She looked at me nervously, I 
smiled back at her, not entirely sincerely, we talked around the 
only thing we wished to talk about. | mentioned a common friend, 
she asked something unrelated, we chatted about the weather and 
the room. Whenever I came closer to her, whenever I drew away, 
I could feel her watching me; the problem, I think, was that she 
seemed an intriguing presence to me (and I, perhaps, not quite 
the sadist she'd imagined). 

Three days later we found ourselves sharing a stage again, and 


this time, just before we went out to face the audience, she told 


me, more than a little anxiously, that she planned to read a piece 
that mentioned my attack on her. She did so—as I'm sure I would 
have done in the same position—and then returned to her seat 


beside me. I said something truthful and peacemaking, she mur- 


mured some general kindness about what I'd just read, we looked 
at each other, looked away. 

The very next day I flew back to Japan and the rural suburb 
where I live, and almost as soon as I got home, I received a mes- 


sage attached to a handle I'd never seen before (and including 


the odd word thing). Inside was a long, unparagraphed, naked out- 
pouring from the woman I'd once attacked—part apology, part 


explanation, part offering of peace. I wrote instantly, with- 


out a thought (as e-mail teaches me to do), and she wrote back 


with an equally heedless speed. I answered, she answered, impa- 


tience was in the air. Suddenly, after nine years of saying nothing, 


we were breathing down each other's nec 

Flung-out, unrehearsed, almost visceral, the messages flew 
back and forth 
long history of trust. On the phone, I realized, I'd have been re- 


cross the Pacific as if between intimates with a 


duced to stammers; ina letter I'd have waited for the diplomatic 
moment; even a fax machine would have slowed us down. But 
online we were pushed, pushed, pushed into a close, dark cham- 
ber where nothing existed but her words, mine, and hers again. 

Then, as suddenly as it had begun, the conversation stopped, 
seemingly forever, Before I assumed my online identity, I'd never 
typed a letter in my life; I'd always gone into a private place, thought 
tibbled off hieroglyphics 
that even I, as often as not, couldn't read. And growing up in Eng- 


of the person I was addressing, and then s 


land, I'd been taught to keep things at z 
I felt by saying nothing. But e-mail has no time for distance, and 


safe distance, and say what 


the cyberworld is about the elimination of all borders and restraints. 
Moments and moods pour through me and I become a different 
self—more metallic, more brittle, my Luddite self assures me—as 


soon as I’m on-screen; my friends receive messages from some- 


one they've never seen and may not even like. 
1 thought about all the things the woman and I hadn't 


din person; I thought about the furious closeness that 
had come out online, and I wondered: Is there something 
to be said for limits?

PAPA’S 
PRIMER 
By Sue Miller 


‘ma fiction writer. Because a student in a writing class 
I was teaching at M.I,T. once told me that research in- 
dicated that writing on a computer changes certain as- 
pects of a person’s style, I still write my first drafts in 
longhand, in bound notebooks, as I always have. The 
transition to the computer, even just for revision, was 
seismic for me—something I came to only reluctantly. Even 
now I print out each version of whatever I’m working on and 
do my revisions in pen or pencil first: scratchy question marks 
and comments in the margins as I edit myself, underlines un- 


der passages that need cutting or expansion, and then, drifting 


down the margin and often over to the back of the sheet, new 


passages in longhand, new bits of dialogue, new—always bet- 
ter, | hope—chunks of description. 

Messy. Time-consuming, Old-fashioned. I concede every- 
thing. But I still benefit enormously from the computer as 
I type in all these changes. How easy it is now! Inserting any- 
thing, anywhere—a breeze. Moving whole chapters or sections 
thereof, simple. But I don’t need to convince you. 

Still, long after I'd convinced myself of the computer's use- 
fulness, I continued to resist the notion of the Internet. No way! 
I thought. Not for me, purist of some sort that I am. 

Then one day I was discussing out-of-print books with a friend 


wwwyil.com September 2001 


135 


who found at an online bookstore something she'd long been 


yearning to own. And I thought at once of my father. He had 
died several years earlier, of Alzheimer’s disease. (It was because I 
was writing about him that I was doing online research on that 
illness.) My father was a church historian, and he'd written a 
number of books on that topic over the course of his life. | owned 
several of them, given to me by him years before, but I didn’t have 
his first one, written when he was a young professor and published 
the year I was born. And I had assumed the possibility of owning 
this book was lost to me forever, Even as I typed in its title at the 
online bookstore, | still assumed that, really. 

But there was his name! Not lost, not gone, remembered even 
in this new technological world: James Hastings Nichols. And there 
were his books—all of them, Though they were all, of course, out 
of print, with what was called “limited availability.” Indeed, | thought, 
still skeptical; but I entered my request anyway. 

There was a kind of promise made in response. Essentially 
they would try to find the book I wanted; at best it might take 
some weeks, 

It took much longer than that. It took long enough so that 
I mostly forgot about it, except for the occasional odd specula- 
tion when I was thinking of my father: Wonder if I'll ever get that 
book of his I ordered. 

And then one beautiful summer day in Vermont I walked 
down my dusty driveway to the metal mailbox on the road. Stand- 


ing in the shade of the birch trees, | opened its flap and pulled out, 


along with a few letters, the flat package in cardboard that had 
come to mean “book” to me, As I walked slowly back to the house, 
trailed by my dogs, the rest of the mail tucked under my arm, I 
pulled the tab that split the cardboard wrapper. 

And there it was, Slim. Its cover dark green. Primer for Protestants, 


by James Hastings Nichols. I stopped in the driveway and opened it. 


It was the first edition, published in my birth year. Clearly it 


had belonged to someone like my father—someone who read pas- 


sionately, editorially, Someone who wrote in his books with pen- 
cil. Here a sentence on the yellowing pages was underlined. Here 
there was a question mark, an exclamation mark in the margin. 
Here the reader had made a pondering note to himself that trailed 
down the side of the page. 

I felt that I had been connected to my father again—through 
his printed words, through the old, well-worn book itself, even 


through the notations this other reader or writer had made in it. 


I felt that this circuitous, ephemeral, oddly modern route, the 
Internet—which had until this moment seemed so remote and 
disconnected from my own life—had affirmed something 
permanent and enduring for me; and standing there under the 
cloudless blue sky of a Vermont afternoon, my heart lifted in 


gratitude for it 


ILY PICTURES. HER FORTHCOMING 
BOOK IS THE WORLD BELOW. 


THANK YOU 
FOR SURFING 


By Christopher Buckley 


ow many Republicans does it take to change a 
lightbulb? 
Three—one to change it, two to reminisce about how 
good the old one was, 
As a conservative, I firmly believe that any 
thing new is bad, or possibly even evil. Unless 
it will lower my cholesterol without my having to exercise, get 
me from New York to L.A. faster, or record an entire season of 
The Sopranos. 

I scoffed at the fax machine. I gave it a year—tops. Then I 
bought one. I pronounced cell phones a fad. 1 darkly murmured 
my assent to the rumor that they caused brain tumors, Now | 
become hysterical if my Ericsson T28 takes longer than 45 sec- 
onds to find a local network in Barcelona. I do, however, still de- 
nounce the use of cell phones by women driving two-ton SUVs. 

The arrival of the Net presented an ontological challenge of a 
higher order. At first I saw clearly that it was a gimmick, quite likely 
evil, and would lead to the end of civilization. When I learned that 
Charles Manson had his own Web site, I said, “See?” 

Patient people sat me down and showed me what else it could 
do. I leaned forward in my chair, “Hmm,” I said. “Hmm.” | was 
soon a willing, if guilty, captive of this cyber Satan, At least as a con- 
servative I was able to comfort myself with the knowledge that the 
Net was invented by the Department of Defense, as a means of 
linking together scientists who spent their days thinking about how 
to blow up the Russkies before they could blow us up. As a (very 
bad) Catholic, I was hugely cheered to find an essay by my hero 
Tom Wolfe about the Jesuit priest Pierre Teilhard de Chardin stat- 
ing that were Chardin still alive today he would probably consider 
the Net, this miraculous thing that links together every human be- 
ing on the planet in the greatest convergence in history, a mani 
festation of God's master plan “to turn all the world...into a single 
sublime Holy Spirit.” What a relief. 

The moments we have had together, the Net and I! To be sure, 
they divide into two distinct categories: Naughty and Otherwise. 


Needless to say, | firmly disapprove of the naughty uses of the Net. 
I'm shocked—shocked!—to find Pamela Anderson streaming video 
and the Anna Nicole Smith House of Worship on my screen. How- 
ever did they get there? Then there was that evening some years ago 
when just before setting off for a dinner party, I checked a site I 


had just discovered Continued on page 151

SPECIAL ADVERTISING SECTION 


{ ncernet music—it’s not just for your PC anymore. 
|) Just a short time ago, digital music was confined 
5 to either your PC, or a portable, walkman-like 
device. Now, there’s a slew of new players on the mar- 
ket that let you capture and listen to digital music just 
about anywhere, be it in your car, in your living room, 
attached to your cell phone, or hanging around your 
neck. In fact, according to eBrain Market Research, 
last year, portable audio devices showed the highest 
growth rate of any type of audio system, with revenues 
climbing 16 percent over the previous year. Market 
research firm International Data Corp. predicts that 
US. shipments of compressed audio players will reach 


18 million in 2005 (and that’s from 2.8 million last 
year). So what’s all the hype about? Read on! 


Why make just a musical statement when you can 
make a fashion statement too? That just may be the 
thinking behind a whole new category of music-to-go 
devices—wearable MP3 players. Consumer electronics 
giant Panasonic has made quite the bold statement in 
this burgeoning market with its new e-wear SD Audio 
Player devices. These ultra-hip devices come in two 
different flavors: the SV-SD75 Audio Player, which is 


Give your whole music collection an upgrade. 


SPECIAL ADVERTISING SECTION 


INTERNET MUSIC 
EVERYWHERE 


about the size of a matchbook and half an inch thick; 
and the SV-SD05 Audio Player Headset, which, 
as its name suggests, is an MP3 player that’s totally 
integrated into a set of headphones. Both sell for 
about $400. 


The SV-SD75 boasts a slick, high-tech design, housed 
in goldtone metal with blue 


accents. It has a 2-line, 
12-character LCD on the front that displays silver 
characters on a blue background, so it’s easy to read. 
This LCD can show song title, artist name, and play 
lise name, so you'll know at a glance what you're 
grooving to. The SV-SD75 also has play/stop, 
skip/search, EQ, play mode, and volume up/down 
keys; as well as a hold/mode/normal dial; so working 
your way through your playlist is easy. Panasonic 
includes a raft of accessories with this player, 
like clip-on headphones with an extension cord, 
a rechargeable battery with charger, and a USB 
reader/writer for song transfers to and from your PC. 


You also get a neck strap and an arm band with a 
multipurpose clip, so you really can wear this device 
however you want. And since it weighs less than two 
ounces (without battery), you'll hardly know you've 


got it on in the first place. 


If you hate dangling wires, you'll love Panasonic’s 
SV-SD05, an MP3 player that’s totally integrated into 
a set of headphones. There’s nothing at all to carry: 
just clamp them on your head, and you're ready to 
rock and roll—literally. To control your music, you 


can use the play/stop and skip/search keys on one side 
of the headset; and the power, volume, and EQ keys 
on the other side. This headset even folds up for easy 
transport or storage (carrying case included) when 
youre not using it. Like its strap-on brother, the 
SV-SD05 has an LCD screen; this one is smaller, so it 
shows the track number and the playlist number only. 


Both Panasonic e-wear devices have a lot of features in 
common. They have a built-in MP3 decoder, so they 
can play MP3 files without transcoding; and they 
have AAC (Advanced Audio Coding) decoders as 
well. The e-wear products also both have XBS (Extra 
Bass System), and random play and repeat play 
features. But perhaps the most cutting-edge technology 
that you'll find in these gadgets is a new type of 
storage called Secure Digital. SD storage cards are 
tiny, postage-stamp-sized devices that are secure 
(as their name implies), so copy-protected music can 
be distributed safely. 


The popularity of Internet music has brought some 
unlikely players into the game as well. Sports outfitter 
Nike offers the $299 psa[play120 (‘“psa” for portable 
sport audio), a MP3 and WMA player that’s been 
specifically designed for sport. This device is tiny and 
oval-shaped, so it’s comfortable and unobtrusive, 
whether you put it in your pocket or strap it on with 
the optional armband or belt. This version can play 
120 minutes of digital music; a 60-minute version is 
available for $199. Nike even sells short sport tops, 
mesh tees, and shorts for women that are specifically 
designed for use with psa[play devices. Samsung also 
has a new wearable player called the Mini Yepp. 
Priced at just $99, this player comes bundled with 
full-length audio tracks from Blues Traveler and 
Samantha Mumba, among others. 


If you're a true technogeek, check out the WMP-1V 
Audio Wrist Watch from Casio ($250). As the name 
implies, it’s a fully functioning watch with alarm, 
stopwatch, hourly time signal, and calendar features. 
But this watch also has an MP3 player built in. Using 
the 32MB MultiMediaCard (nonreplaceable), you 
can listen to up to 66 minutes of music (or 33 
minutes on the high-quality setting) via headphones, 
which plug into the left side of the watch. 


PLAY IT AT HOME 


Internet music also is making its way into your home, 
but with some devices, you don’t even need a PC to 
get your MP3. One such product is the $1,200 


AudioReQuest ARQ1-30 
AudioReQuest ARQI-30 from ReQuest Multimedia, 


which is a home stereo component, so it will fit into 
your home entertainment system. This device is a 
combination CD player, MP3 and WMA player, 
and jukebox that can store up to 525 hours of digital 
music from the Internet, or encoded music from your 
favorite CD, LP, cassette, MiniDisc, or even 8-track 


via stereo analog line input. S-Video and composite 


video-out jacks allow you to hook the device to your 


TV set, so you can edit playlists. 


The AudioReQuest also has parallel and USB 
connectors for easy song transfer to and from your PC 


or portable MP3 player; as well as an Ethernet port, 


so you can connect it to your home network. You can 


even trans 


er images or digital photos from your PC 
and display them on a connected television for a true 
multimedia experience. This product is a fabulous 
example of the device conversion that you will 
increasingly see across all types of consumer electronics 


product categories in the future. 


AudioRamp iRAD-S 


Another such device is the $599 AudioRamp 
iRAD-S, the first shelf-top “Internet stereo.” ‘There 
are many audio mini-systems on the market, but none 
quite like this one. It combines traditional stereo 
features like a CD player and AM/FM radio with the 
ability to play Internet radio stations in either WMA 
or MP3 streaming format. The device comes 
preloaded with hundreds of Internet radio stations in 


different formats, so it’s ready to go right out of 
the box. To connect the iRAD to the Internet, 
you can use either a 56K modem line, Ethernet, 
or HomePNA—all are supported. 


To add or update station information, you can use 
the personalized MyAudioRamp Web site, which 
dynamically retrieves the content that you want on a 
daily basis. The system also has a hard drive inside, so 
you can store over 1,000 songs at a time, or record 
music from CDs directly to the drive. The iRAD-S 
uses what AudioRamp calls IntelligentAudio 
, which allows you to interact with content 


technolog) 
while you listen. Using the ActionButton, you can 
request. news headlines, buy CDs, or even order 
concert tickets. You can also use it to take a “digital 
fingerprint” of a song you're listening to on the 
radio—just push the button during the song, and 
IntelligentAudio will tell you the artist’s name, song 
title, and label; store that information; and gather 
further related information from different Web sites. 
It’s features like these that take the listening 


experience to an entirely new level. 


Even traditional “PC” companies are getting into this 
game, realizing that digital music has become an 
integral part of the computing experience. Both Dell 


and Gateway have been selling what can loosely be 


called digital jukebox appliances since last year; both 
can connect to a home network in order to pipe 


digital music from your PC to any location in the 


house. Gateway’s product is called the Connected 
Music Player, and sells for $299 (HomePNA and 
Ethernet ready); Dell’s is the Digital Audio Receiver, 
which costs $149 for the unit alone, or $199 in 
various bundles that let you connect it to a network 
via Ethernet or HPNA. And, by the time you read 
this, Compaq’s new iPAQ Music Center will be 
available, which goes one better than Dell and 
Gateway by adding the ability to listen to Internet 


radio stations to its device.

before the two AAA batteries run out. 
The Pocket Concert supports MP3 
and WMA formats now, but its 
firmware is reprogrammable, so it 
can be compatible with more file 
formats as they're introduced in the 


future. The device also has a built-in 
FM radio with 10 presets. Optional 
accessories include a Stereo Dock and Intel Pocket Concert 
a Car Adapter, so the Pocket Concert 


can be used in a variety of locations. 


One of the early pioneers in this market, Creative Labs, has introduced a new line 
of devices that take portable audio to the next level. The Nomad Jukebox systems 


are about the size of portable CD players, and have 6GB worth of storage, so they 
can hold the same number of songs as 150 CDs (at 128kbps MP3 encoding; up 
to 300 at 64kbps WMA). The new Nomad Jukebox C is priced at just $269; 
supports MP3, WMA, and WAV format 
external devices via its stereo line-in minijack. The player also supports four- 


; and you can record directly to it from 


channel sound via two line-out jacks for front and rear speakers; and it has a memory 


buffer that’s large enough to handle up to five minutes of shock protection. 
The Nomad Jukebox can easily double as a portable player and as your primary 
music device at home—perfect for small spaces like New York C' 


apartments. 


Providing yet another twist on the portable MP3 theme is Samsung, with its 
YVP-P300, the Photo Yepp II. Due to be released later this year, the Photo Yepp II 
will have a full-color, 1.8-inch LCD screen, so you can display still images and 
lyrics to accompany the songs that you're listening to. The device can play MP3 
or WMA formats, has 64MB of internal flash memory, and will accept 
SmartMedia cards as well. 


No matter how you choose to get your MP3s—be it in your living room, 
in your car, in the palm of your hand, or strapped to your arm—your choices 
have never been more varied or exciting. If you're a music fan (and who isn't?) 
you owe it to yourself to check out these and other devices, so you too can join 
the MP3 revolution. &

The Consumer Electronics 
Association (CEA) is the trade 
organization for the companies 
at create and market t 
products that enable the 
information age. Best known 
as the producer and sponsor 
of the annual International 
Consumer Electronics Show 
in Las Vegas each winter, 
CEA reinvests all profits from 
CES into industry services 
including technical training 
and education, product promo- 
tion, engineering standards 
development, market research, 
and governmental and legal 
affairs support. CEA provides 
resources to help you navigate 
the fast-moving consumer 
technology industry, where 
products continue to converge— 
blurring the lines between 
information technology and 
entertainment, between televi- 
sion and computer, between 
lifestyle and workstyle, and 
between e-commerce and 
traditional retail stores.

By Modern Humorist 


Imodernhumorist.com] 


Christopher Buckley 


called the Drudge Report and found this item 
about a blue dress. I was the hit of the party. 

As for the Otherwise moments: There 
was the summer when, in our fragile little 
cabin on the rocky Maine coast I sought to 
find out the track of the hurricane that was 
working its furious way up the American 
littoral to see if 1, my wife, and our babies 
were in its path. I soon found a site that 
provided not only real-time satellite pho- 
tos of the storm, but also its probable track 
and estimated time of landfall. | rubbed 
my eyes like Cro-Magnon man beholding 
fire for the first time. 

Someone said to me, “Do you know 
about concordance.com?” 

“Tell me about it!” I cried. “I want to 
know!” I had been transformed from grumpy 
Luddite to avid Futurist. 

Great Books: Tests and Fully Searchable Con- 
cordances [concordance.coml is the work of a 
retired actuary—saintly man, cyberpriest- 
who has created a site gathering together the 
concordances of many major authors and works 
of literature (my own, conspicuously excluded). 
Want to know how many times Huck Finn 
used the n-word? In which scene in King Lear 
occurs the phrase, “How sharper than a se 
pent's tooth...”? Where in the Bible it 


liciously, “comfort me with apples”? Go to 
Great Books and give grateful thanks, pilgrim 

Someone else said to me, “Do you know 
about Trip.com?” Before he could say another 
word, I was there, pricing flights and 


renting cars. What's this little link here? 


Flight Tracker? You're picking up your wife 
at the airport tonight. She’s coming in on 
Delta Flight 17 from Salt Lake. Type it in. 


Wait...10 seconds—an eterr 


Dammit, can’t you go any 
go, an icon of her plane, superimposed above 
Indiana. She’s running 20 minutes late. 
Still have time to finish supper before head- 
ing off to Dulles. Wow gives way to a shrug, 
No, I lie. I am still Cro-Magnon man. My 
jaw scrapes the ground. 

What is the Dr. Seuss title I read to my 
babies? Oh, the Places You'll Go! People today 
say, “Don't go there.” But I go! I’m there! 

How many Republicans does it take to 
do a Google search? 

Three—one to hit Search, two to reminisce about 


how slow the old modem was. <<
